Generalized cluster structures on SL n † \SL_{n}^{\dagger}
Dmitriy Voloshyn, Michael Gekhtman

Abstract
We study Poisson varieties ( SL n † , π 𝚪 † ) (\SL_{n}^{\dagger},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}) parameterized by Belavin-Drinfeld triples 𝚪 \mathbf{\Gamma} of type A n − 1 A_{n-1} along with generalized cluster structures 𝒢 ​ 𝒞 † ​ ( 𝚪 ) \mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}) on SL n † \SL_{n}^{\dagger} compatible with π 𝚪 † \pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger} . As a variety, SL n † \SL_{n}^{\dagger} is a Zariski open subset of SL n \SL_{n} obtained as the image of the dual Poisson-Lie group ( SL n ∗ , π 𝚪 ∗ ) (\SL_{n}^{*},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{*}) of ( SL n , π 𝚪 ) (\SL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}) under a certain rational map. We prove that the generalized upper cluster structure of 𝒢 ​ 𝒞 † ​ ( 𝚪 ) \mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}) on SL n \SL_{n} is naturally isomorphic to 𝒪 ⁡ ( SL n ) \mathcal{O}(\SL_{n}) . Moreover, for any connected reductive complex group G G and a BD triple 𝚪 \mathbf{\Gamma} , we produce a rational map 𝒬 : ( G , π std † ) ⇢ ( G , π 𝚪 † ) \mathcal{Q}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}) that is a Poisson isomorphism if the r 0 r_{0} parts of π std † \pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger} and π 𝚪 † \pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger} coincide; furthermore, in the case when G ∈ { SL n , GL n } G\in\{\SL_{n},\GL_{n}\} , we show that 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is a birational quasi-isomorphism between 𝒢 ​ 𝒞 † ​ ( 𝚪 std ) \mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}) and 𝒢 ​ 𝒞 † ​ ( 𝚪 ) \mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}) . Lastly, for any pair of BD triples 𝚪 ~ ≺ 𝚪 \tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma} of type A n − 1 A_{n-1} comparable in the natural order, we use the map 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} to construct a birational quasi-isomorphism between 𝒢 ​ 𝒞 † ​ ( 𝚪 ~ ) \mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}) and 𝒢 ​ 𝒞 † ​ ( 𝚪 ) \mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}) .

Key words and phrases.
Poisson-Lie group, cluster algebra, Belavin-Drinfeld triple.

1
Introduction

1.1
Overview

Cluster algebras were introduced by S. Fomin and A. Zelevinsky in
[
13
]
as an algebraic framework for studying dual canonical bases and total positivity. S. Fomin and A. Zelevinsky suggested that many interesting varieties in Lie theory carry the structure of an (upper) cluster algebra. Some of the known examples include double Bruhat cells
[
3
]
, Grassmannians
[
36
]
, Bott-Samelson cells
[
37
]
, connected simply connected simple complex algebraic groups
[
18
]
, open Richardson varieties
[
28
]
.

M. Gekhtman, M. Shapiro and A. Vainshtein noticed in
[
17
]
that the problem of finding cluster structures on varieties can be approached via Poisson geometry. In the earlier works
[
17
,
18
,
24
]
, they showed that the cluster structures on double Bruhat cells, Grassmannians, connected simply connected complex simple algebraic groups
G
are compatible with some Poisson brackets. They conjectured in
[
18
]
that there exist multiple cluster structures on such groups
G
, each of which is compatible with a Poisson bracket from the Belavin-Drinfeld class. We refer to this conjecture as the
GSV conjecture
.

Before we state the GSV conjecture in precise terms, let us briefly review the relevant concepts from the theory of Poisson-Lie groups and the theory of cluster algebras (a more detailed account can be found in Section
2
). Given a set of simple roots
\Pi
of
G
, a
Belavin-Drinfeld triple
(for short, a
BD triple
) is a triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}:=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
where
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\subset\Pi
and
\gamma:\Gamma_{1}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2}
is a nilpotent isometry. The triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}:=\mathbf{\Gamma}
is called
trivial
if
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset
. Let
\mathfrak{h}
be the Cartan subalgebra of the Lie algebra of
G
that corresponds to
\Pi
. As was shown by Belavin and Drinfeld in
[
1
,
2
]
, with some additional continuous parameter
r_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathfrak{h}
, the quadruples
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma,r_{0})
parameterize the moduli space of the quasi-triangular Poisson structures on
G
. We will denote a Poisson bivector associated to
\mathbf{\Gamma}
(for an arbitrary
r_{0}
) as
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. The Poisson structure on
G
induces a Lie algebra structure on the dual of its Lie algebra; integrating the latter, one obtains a
dual Poisson-Lie group

G^{*}
, which is endowed with its own Poisson bivector
\pi^{*}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. It is possible to choose a Poisson bivector
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{D}
on
D(G):=G\times G
and the dual
G^{*}
in such a way that 1)
G
is the diagonal subgroup of
D(G)
and
G^{*}
is an immersed Lie subgroup of
D(G)
; 2)
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{D}|_{G}=\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
and
\pi^{D}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}|_{G^{*}}=\pi^{*}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. Consider the map

D(G)\ni(g,h)\mapsto g^{-1}h\in G,

(1.1)

and define the Poisson bivector
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
to be the pushforward of
\pi^{D}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
under (
1.1
). The restriction of (
1.1
) to
G^{*}
has an open image in
G
, which is denoted as
G^{\dagger}
. The Poisson structure
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
serves as a model for the Poisson structure
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{*}
inside the group
G
itself. When
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}
, we will denote the corresponding Poisson bivectors as
\pi_{\text{std}}
,
\pi_{\text{std}}^{D}
and
\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger}
. More generally, one can associate Poisson-homogeneous bivectors
\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})}
and
\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})}^{D}
to pairs of Belavin-Drinfeld triples
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
(see
[
23
,
30
,
31
]
).

Now, let us turn to the cluster algebras theory. In the context of the GSV conjecture, one starts with an
initial extended cluster

(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N+M})
, which is a collection of rational functions such that
\mathbb{C}(G)=\mathbb{C}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N+M})
,
N+M=\dim G
. The variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
are called
cluster variables
and
x_{N+1},\ldots,x_{N+M}
are the
frozen variables
. Given a skew-symmetrizable
N\times(N+M)
matrix
B
with integer entries, for each
i\in[1,N]
one can obtain a new extended cluster from the initial one via substituting the cluster variable
x_{i}
with a new cluster variable
x_{i}^{\prime}
given by

x_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}=\prod_{b_{ij}>0}x_{j}^{b_{ij}}+\prod_{b_{ij}<0}x_{j}^{-b_{ij}}.

(1.2)

The above transformation is called a
mutation.
For each new extended cluster, the matrix
B
also changes according to a certain pattern. Iterating the above process, one obtains other extended clusters, and the collection
\mathcal{C}
of those is called a
cluster structure
on
G
. The
cluster algebra

\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}
is set to be the polynomial algebra over
\mathbb{C}
generated by all cluster and frozen variables. With each extended cluster
\mathbf{x}
, we associate a ring of Laurent polynomials
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{N}^{\pm 1},x_{N+1},\ldots,x_{N+M}]
, and we set the upper cluster algebra
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}
to be the intersection of all
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{x})
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{C}
(possibly, with an additional localization at some frozen variables). The famous Laurent phenomenon claims that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}
. We say that
\mathcal{C}
is
regular
if
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(G)
and
\mathcal{C}
is
complete
if
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathcal{O}(G)
(in this case, we also say that
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is
naturally isomorphic
to
\mathcal{O}(G)
). An extended cluster
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N+M})
is called
log-canonical
if
\{x_{i},x_{j}\}=\omega_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}
, where
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is the Poisson bracket that corresponds to the chosen Poisson bivector and
\omega_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}
. If each extended cluster is log-canonical, the cluster algebra (or structure) is said to be
compatible
with the Poisson structure. A generalized cluster structure
\mathcal{GC}
is defined in the same way except (
1.2
) is allowed to have more than two monomials in the right-hand side along with some coefficients.

The GSV conjecture.

In its current form, the conjecture is stated as follows:

Let
G
be a connected simple complex Lie group and
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
be a pair of Belavin-Drinfeld triples defined relative a root system of
G
. Then for each of the Poisson varieties
(G,\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
,
(D(G),\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})}^{D})
and
(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c}}^{\dagger})
, there exists a complete and compatible generalized cluster structure.

The results of our paper cover the case of
(\SL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
for
all
Belavin-Drinfeld triples
1
1

1

The reader might notice that the Poisson structure
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c}}^{\dagger}
depends only on
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c}
and not on the pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
. The reason for that is, the pushforward of
\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})}^{D}
under the map
(X,Y)\mapsto X^{-1}Y
”forgets” the BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r}
.

\mathbf{\Gamma}:=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c}
of type
A_{n-1}
, as well as some related varieties, such as
(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
and
(\SL_{n}^{\dagger},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
. Setting
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r}:=(\Gamma_{1}^{r},\Gamma_{2}^{r},\gamma_{r})
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c}:=(\Gamma_{1}^{c},\Gamma_{2}^{c},\gamma_{c})
, recall that the pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
is called
aperiodic
if the map
\gamma_{r}w_{0}\gamma_{c}^{-1}w_{0}^{-1}
is nilpotent, where
w_{0}
is the longest Weyl group element. In type
A_{n-1}
, identifying the set of simple roots
\Pi
with the interval of integers
[1,n-1]
, the pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
is called
oriented
if both
\gamma_{r}
and
\gamma_{c}
are increasing maps. The current state of the GSV conjecture is as follows.

1)

In the case of
(\SL_{n},\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
, the conjecture was verified for aperiodic pairs
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
in
[
15
]
;

2)

In the case of
(D(\SL_{n}),\pi^{D}_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
, the conjecture was verified for all aperiodic oriented pairs
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
in
[
31
]
;

3)

In the case of
(\SL_{n},\pi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
, the conjecture was verified only for the trivial BD triple in
[
20
]
. The results of the current paper prove the conjecture for all BD triples on
(\SL_{n},\pi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
;

4)

As for
(G,\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
of other Lie types, the conjecture was verified in
[
18
]
only for the trivial pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r}_{\text{std}},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c}_{\text{std}})
. However, even for the trivial BD triples, constructions in the cases of
(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
and
(D(G),\pi^{D}_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
have not been discovered yet.

All the results on
\SL_{n}
can be extended to
\GL_{n}
. The Poisson-homogeneous structures
\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})}
and
\pi^{D}_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})}
were included into the conjecture in
[
23
,
31
]
. Generalized cluster mutations appear in the case of
(\SL_{n},\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
when the pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
is not aperiodic
[
22
]
; in the case of
(D(\SL_{n}),\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
and
(\SL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
, generalized cluster mutations appear even for the trivial Belavin-Drinfeld triples.

Connection to other problems.

We envision a connection between our work and some of the following known problems in mathematical physics:

1)

There is a conjecture due to D. Gaiotto on the existence of cluster structures on Coulomb branches in
\mathcal{N}=4
,
3d
supersymmetric gauge theories. The conjecture was verified in the case of quiver gauge theories by A. Shapiro and G. Schrader in
[
35
]
. It would be interesting to see if
(\SL_{n}^{\dagger},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
for a nontrivial
\mathbf{\Gamma}
can be interpreted as a character variety. For
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}
, the question was studied by R. Brahami in
[
4
]
and L. Shen in
[
38
]
. There are some thoughts in
[
21
, Section 4]
on a relationship to Fock–Goncharov coordinates on moduli spaces of local system
[
10
]
, as well as on a possible interpretation of generalized cluster mutations. If this is done, one might be able to recognize
(\SL_{n}^{\dagger},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
as a Coulomb branch for some physical theory.

2)

G. Schrader, A. Shapiro and I. Ip in
[
26
,
34
]
proposed a quantum cluster
\mathcal{X}
-realization of the quantum group
U_{g}(\mathfrak{g})
for simple complex Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
. As a byproduct, they derived a factorization into quantum dilogarithms of the quantum
R
-matrix associated with the trivial BD triple. An explicit classification of (quasi-triangular) quantum
R
-matrices is well-known and given in
[
9
]
. We expect the following: 1) there exists a connection between compatible generalized cluster structures on
(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
and quantum
\mathcal{A}
- or
\mathcal{X}
-cluster realizations of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
(as above, this requires a realization of
(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
as a character variety); 2) quantum
R
-matrices for nontrivial BD triples can also be factorized into quantum dilogarithms (or their twists).

Some of the intrinsic problems of the theory presented in this paper are listed in our supplementary note
[
33
]
.

Generalized cluster mutations and aperiodicity.

For compatible generalized cluster structures on
(G,\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
, there is a significant distinction between the cases of aperiodic and nonaperiodic BD pairs. In the case of
G=\SL_{n}
and an aperiodic BD pair, the initial cluster variables are obtained as trailing minors of the so-called
\mathcal{L}
-matrices (the definition is given in
[
15
,
23
]
); however, these matrices become infinite when the pair is nonaperiodic. Moreover, at least in the case of
(\SL_{n},\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
, one has to consider generalized cluster mutations when
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
is nonaperiodic (for an example, see
[
22
]
). However, we found that in the case of
(\SL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
, one does not need to distinguish between the aperiodic and nonaperiodic cases, and for any BD triple in our construction, there is always only one generalized cluster mutation.

A new approach.

Compared to the previous papers
[
19
,
23
,
31
]
for nontrivial BD triples, the proofs in the current paper are significantly simpler. Our new approach, which was nascent in
[
23
,
31
]
, consists in studying properties of certain Poisson birational quasi-isomorphisms, which seem to be the right type of equivalences in the context of the GSV conjecture. These maps (almost) preserve all of the considered structures on a given variety; hence, once the results are proved in the case of the trivial Belavin-Drinfeld data, the Poisson birational quasi-isomorphisms can be used to transfer the results to the case of nontrivial BD triples.

1)

Previously, the proofs of compatibility between the chosen Poisson bracket and the (generalized) cluster structure consisted in direct and involved computations
[
19
,
23
,
31
]
. In the current paper, given any connected reductive complex Lie group
G
, any BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and a Poisson bivector
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
on
G
, we prove that a certain rational map
\mathcal{Q}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
is a Poisson isomorphism provided the
r_{0}
parts of the Poisson bivectors are the same (see Section
3
). In the case of
G\in\{\SL_{n},\GL_{n}\}
,
\mathcal{Q}
is a birational quasi-isomorphism. We expect that generalized cluster structures for nontrivial BD triples in other Lie types can be constructed by means of the map
\mathcal{Q}
. The compatibility of
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
with the corresponding generalized cluster structure on
G
is a consequence of the Poisson property of
\mathcal{Q}
.

2)

As for showing that the generalized upper cluster algebra coincides with the ring of regular functions on
\SL_{n}
, we mainly use the results of
[
32
]
and an inductive argument on the size of Belavin-Drinfeld triples, which was suggested in
[
23
]
. The proof is reduced to verifying properties of certain marked variables in the case
|\Gamma_{1}|=1
.

3)

The existence of a global toric action of maximal rank follows from certain equivariance properties of
\mathcal{Q}
, and the proof is reduced to the verification of some properties of the marked variables in the case
|\Gamma_{1}|=1
.

In the case of
(G,\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
, the same approach is developed in
[
15
]
. For every pair of BD triples
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
, the authors constructed a certain rational map
\mathbf{h}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
and showed that
\mathbf{h}
is Poisson if the
r_{0}
parts of
\pi_{\text{std}}
and
\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})}
coincide. As we mentioned above, there is an obstacle in constructing the initial extended cluster on
(G,\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})})
when
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
is not aperiodic. In terms of
\mathbf{h}
, the obstacle translates into the fact that
\mathbf{h}^{-1}
is not rational for a nonaperiodic pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
. However, evidence suggests that by means of
\mathbf{h}^{-1}
, one can transform a cluster structure compatible with
\pi_{\text{std}}
into a generalized cluster structure compatible with
\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})}
. It is the next major question in the research program whether one can combine
\mathbf{h}
and
\mathcal{Q}
into a map that can generate a compatible generalized cluster structure on the Drinfeld double
(D(G),\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})}^{D})
.

Organization of the paper.

In Section
1.2
, we provide a description of the initial extended seed in the
h
-convention
(an alternative construction, which we refer to as the
g
-convention
, is given in Section
7
). In Section
1.3
, we state the main results of the paper. In Section
2
, we provide an expanded background on Poisson geometry, generalized cluster theory and the theory of birational quasi-isomorphisms. In Section
3
, we construct the rational map
\mathcal{Q}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
for any connected reductive complex Lie group
G
, and then we prove that
\mathcal{Q}
is a Poisson isomorphism provided the
r_{0}
parts of
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
and
\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger}
are the same. In Section
4
, we construct various birational quasi-isomorphisms in the case of
G\in\{\SL_{n},\GL_{n}\}
. In Section
5
, we prove our main Theorems
1.5
-
1.6
. In Section
6
, we derive an explicit formula for the variables from the initial extended cluster, as well as prove special Poisson properties of the frozen variables. Some explicit examples of the generalized cluster structures on
(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
are listed in Appendix
D
.

Acknowledgements.

The first author was supported by the grant IBS-R003-D1. The second author was supported by the NSF grant 2100785.

1.2
Construction of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})

Let
\Pi
be a fixed set of simple roots of type
A_{n-1}
identified with the interval
[1,n-1]
. In this subsection, given a BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}:=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
, we describe the initial extended cluster of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
on
(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
and
(\SL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
in the
h
-convention
. The ground field is always assumed to be
\mathbb{C}
. For the trivial BD triple, the description was provided in
[
20
]
. In Section
7
we provide an alternative construction that we refer to as the
g
-convention
. The initial extended cluster comprises three types of functions:
c
-functions,
\varphi
-functions and
h
-functions. Only the description of the
h
-functions depends on the choice of the Belavin-Drinfeld triple. The description of the initial quiver is provided in Appendix
A
, and selected examples are provided in Appendix
D
.

Notation for
\mathcal{GC}
.

The notation
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
stands for the generalized cluster structure on
(\SL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
or
(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
in the
h
- or
g
-convention. The lower script
h
or
g
will specify the convention. If we need to specify a variety, we will write
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n})
or
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\SL_{n})
. The same pattern will be applied for the (upper) generalized cluster algebra, the ground ring, etc. The notation
(G,\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}))
(with or without the subscript
h
or
g
) reads as a group
G\in\{\SL_{n},\GL_{n}\}
supplied with
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},G)
. The choice of
r_{0}
is not relevant for the construction.

Description of
\varphi
- and
c
-functions.

For an element
U\in\GL_{n}
, let us set

\Phi_{kl}(U):=\begin{bmatrix}(U^{0})^{[n-k+1,n]}&U^{[n-l+1,n]}&(U^{2})^{\{n\}}&\cdots&(U^{n-k-l+1})^{\{n\}}\end{bmatrix},\ \ k,l\geq 1,\ k+l\leq n;

(1.3)

s_{kl}:=\begin{cases}(-1)^{k(l+1)}\ &n\ \text{is even},\\
(-1)^{(n-1)/2+k(k-1)/2+l(l-1)/2}\ &n\ \text{is odd}.\end{cases}

(1.4)

Then the
\varphi
-functions are given by

\varphi_{kl}(U):=s_{kl}\det\Phi_{kl}(U).

(1.5)

The
\varphi
-functions enjoy the following invariance properties. Given a unipotent lower triangular matrix
N_{-}
and a nondegenerate diagonal matrix
T
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{kl}(N_{-}U\,\hskip-3.0pt\left.N_{-}\hskip-4.0pt\right.^{-1})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{kl}(U),

(1.6)

\displaystyle\varphi_{kl}(TUT^{-1})

\displaystyle=\chi_{\varphi_{kl}}(T)\varphi_{kl}(U)

where
\chi_{\varphi_{kl}}
is a character. The
c
-functions are uniquely defined via

\det(I+\lambda U)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\lambda^{i}s_{i}c_{i}(U)

(1.7)

where
s_{i}:=(-1)^{i(n-1)}
and
I
is the identity matrix. Note that
c_{0}=I
and
c_{n}=\det U
.

Remark 1.1
.

Let
D(\GL_{n}):=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
be the Drinfeld double of
\GL_{n}
. For
(X,Y)\in D(\GL_{n})
, set
U:=X^{-1}Y
. Then the definition of the
\varphi
- and
c
-functions is (up to a factor of
\det X
) identical to the definitions provided in the previous papers
[
20
,
31
]
. The functions
\varphi_{kl}
also coincide with the functions
\psi_{kl}
described in
[
20
]
for
\SL_{n}^{\dagger}
in the case of the trivial Belavin-Drinfeld triple.

Description of the
h
-functions.

For any generic
n\times n
matrix
A
, let us denote by
A_{\oplus}
and
A_{-}
the upper triangular and the unipotent lower triangular matrices, respectively, such that
A=A_{\oplus}A_{-}
. For a given BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
of type
A_{n-1}
, the map
\gamma^{*}
can be extended to a Lie algebra homomorphism
\gamma^{*}:\mathfrak{n}_{-}(\Gamma_{2})\rightarrow\mathfrak{n}_{-}(\Gamma_{1})
, where
\mathfrak{n}_{-}(\Gamma_{i})
are the subalgebras of
\sll_{n}(\mathbb{C})
of nilpotent lower triangular matrices generated by
\Gamma_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. Then
\gamma^{*}
can be extended by zero to the map
\gamma^{*}:\mathfrak{n}_{-}\rightarrow\mathfrak{n}_{-}
, which is still a Lie algebra homomorphism. Lastly, it can be integrated to a group homomorphism
\tilde{\gamma}^{*}:\mathcal{N}_{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{-}
, where
\mathcal{N}_{-}
is the subgroup of
\SL_{n}
of unipotent lower triangular matrices (see Section
2.1
and Section
4.1
for details). Define the following sequence of rational maps:

\mathcal{F}_{0}(U):=U,\ \ \mathcal{F}_{k}(U):=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([\mathcal{F}_{k-1}(U)]_{-})\cdot U,\ \ U\in\GL_{n},\ k\geq 1.

(1.8)

We study properties of
\{\mathcal{F}_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
in Section
4.2
. The sequence stabilizes due to the nilpotency of
\gamma
:

\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma}(U)=\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma+1}(U)=\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma+2}=\ldots

(1.9)

(for
\deg\gamma
, see Definition
4.1
). Set

\mathcal{F}(U):=\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma}(U),\ \ U\in\GL_{n}.

(1.10)

Definition 1.2
.

For
\alpha_{0}\in\Pi\setminus\Gamma_{2}
, set
\alpha_{t}:=\gamma(\alpha_{t-1})
,
t\geq 1
. The sequence
S^{\gamma}(\alpha_{0}):=\{\alpha_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is called the
\gamma
-string
associated to
\alpha_{0}
.

Note that
\gamma
-strings partition the set of simple roots
\Pi
. For each
\gamma
-string
S^{\gamma}(\alpha_{0})=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}\}
, for each
i\in[0,m]
and
j\in[\alpha_{i}+1,n]
, set

h_{\alpha_{i}+1,j}(U):=(-1)^{\varepsilon_{\alpha_{i}+1,j}}\det[\mathcal{F}(U)]^{[j,n]}_{[\alpha_{i}+1,n-j+\alpha_{i}+1]}\prod_{t\geq i+1}^{m}\det[\mathcal{F}(U)]^{[\alpha_{t}+1,n]}_{[\alpha_{t}+1,n]}

(1.11)

where
\varepsilon_{ij}
is defined as

\varepsilon_{ij}:=(j-i)(n-i),\ \ 1\leq i\leq j\leq n.

(1.12)

We refer to the functions
h_{ij}
,
2\leq i\leq j\leq n
, together with
h_{11}(U):=\det U
as the
h
-functions
. Note that the flag minors of
\mathcal{F}(U)
can be expressed as

(-1)^{\varepsilon_{ij}}\det[\mathcal{F}(U)]_{[i,n-j+i]}^{[j,n]}=\begin{cases}\frac{h_{ij}(U)}{h_{\mu\mu}(U)}\ &\text{if}\ i-1\in\Gamma_{1},\\
h_{ij}(U)\ &\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(1.13)

where
\mu:=\gamma(i-1)+1
for
i-1\in\Gamma_{1}
. When
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}
, we obtain that

h_{ij}(U)=(-1)^{\varepsilon_{ij}}\det U^{[j,n]}_{[i,n-j+i]},\ \ 2\leq i\leq j\leq n,

(1.14)

which coincides with the earlier description given in
[
20
]
. We will show in Section
6.2
that the
h
-functions are regular as elements of
\mathcal{O}(\GL_{n})
, and we will also derive an explicit formula in terms of minors of
U\in\GL_{n}
. Moreover, in Section
5.1.3
we will show that the
h
-functions are irreducible and coprime with their mutations.

Frozen variables.

In the case of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n})
, the frozen variables are given by the set

\{c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{n-1}\}\cup\{h_{i+1,i+1}\ |\ i\in\Pi\setminus\Gamma_{2}\}\cup\{h_{11}\}.

(1.15)

In the case of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\SL_{n})
,
h_{11}(U)=1
, so this variable is absent. We will show in Section
6.4
that the zero loci of the frozen variables foliate into unions of symplectic leaves of the ambient Poisson variety
(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
or
(\SL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
. In the same section, we will also show that the frozen
h
-variables do not vanish on
\SL_{n}^{\dagger}
.

Initial extended cluster.

The initial extended cluster
\Psi_{0}
of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n})
is given by the set

\{h_{ij}\ |\ 2\leq i\leq j\leq n\}\cup\{\varphi_{kl}\ |\ k,l\geq 1,\ k+l\leq n\}\cup\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1}\}\cup\{h_{11}\}.

(1.16)

The initial extended cluster of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\SL_{n})
is obtained from
\Psi_{0}
via removing
h_{11}
.

Ground rings.

The ground rings are defined as follows.

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n}):=

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[h_{11}^{\pm 1},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1},h_{i+1,i+1}\ |\ i\in\Pi\setminus\Gamma_{2}];

(1.17)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\SL_{n}):=

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1},h_{i+1,i+1}\ |\ i\in\Pi\setminus\Gamma_{2}];

(1.18)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\SL_{n}^{\dagger}):=

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1},h_{i+1,i+1}^{\pm 1}\ |\ i\in\Pi\setminus\Gamma_{2}].

(1.19)

Note that the localizations in the ground rings reflect the fact that the corresponding frozen variables, as regular functions on the corresponding varieties, are nonvanishing. In this paper, we prove the results for
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n})
and
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\SL_{n})
, and the results for
\SL_{n}^{\dagger}
and
\GL_{n}^{\dagger}
follow from localizing the ground rings. In the case of
\GL_{n}^{\dagger}
, the choice of the ground ring is subtle (see Remark
6.11
).

A generalized cluster mutation.

In the initial extended cluster, only the variable
\varphi_{11}
is equipped with a nontrivial
string
, which is given by
(1,c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1},1)
(see Definition
2.6
). The generalized mutation relation for
\varphi_{11}
reads

\varphi_{11}\varphi_{11}^{\prime}=\sum_{r=0}^{n}c_{r}\varphi_{21}^{r}\varphi_{12}^{n-r}.

(1.20)

Other mutations of the initial extended cluster follow the usual pattern from the theory of cluster algebras of geometric type.

Toric action.

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be the Cartan subalgebra of
\sll_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. Set

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}:=\{h\in\mathfrak{h}\ |\ \alpha(h)=\beta(h)\ \text{if}\ \gamma^{j}(\alpha)=\beta\ \text{for some}\ j\}.

(1.21)

The dimension of
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is given by

k_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}:=\dim_{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}=|\Pi\setminus\Gamma_{2}|.

(1.22)

Set
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
to be the connected subgroup of
\SL_{n}
that corresponds to
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. We let
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
to act upon
\GL_{n}
and
\SL_{n}
via

(H,U)\mapsto HUH^{-1},\ \ H\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\ U\in\GL_{n}.

(1.23)

In the case of
\GL_{n}
, we will consider the action of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
, where
\mathbb{C}^{*}
acts as a multiplication by scalars:

(a,U)\mapsto a\cdot U,\ \ a\in\mathbb{C}^{*},\ U\in\GL_{n}.

(1.24)

In Section
5.2
we will show that the action of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
upon
\SL_{n}
and the action of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
upon
\GL_{n}
induce global toric actions upon the corresponding generalized cluster structures.

The Poisson bracket.

A general formula for the Poisson bracket is given in (
2.18
). In the cases of
\GL_{n}
and
\SL_{n}
, the Poisson bracket that corresponds to
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
is given as follows. First, for a smooth function
f\in C^{\infty}(\GL_{n})
, set

\nabla_{U}f:=\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial u_{ij}}\right)_{j,i=1}^{n},\ \ U\in\GL_{n};

(1.25)

second, equip
\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and
\sll_{n}(\mathbb{C})
with the trace form

\langle A,B\rangle:=\tr(AB),\ \ A,B\in\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

Let
R:=R_{+}:\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
be the classical
R
-matrix that corresponds to
\mathbf{\Gamma}
(see formula (
2.7
) below). The Poisson bracket on
(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
is given by

\{f,g\}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}=\langle R([\nabla_{U}f,U]),[\nabla_{U}g,U]\rangle-\langle[\nabla_{U}f,U],\nabla_{U}g\cdot U\rangle.

(1.26)

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is trivial, we will denote the corresponding Poisson bracket as
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\text{std}}^{\dagger}
. In the case of
(\SL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
, the formula is exactly the same, except one chooses
R
as a linear endomorphism of
\sll_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

Casimirs.

For any
2
2

2

In our work on the Drinfeld double
[
31
]
, the functions
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1}
defined on
(D(\GL_{n}),\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{D})
are Casimirs if and only if
R_{0}(I)=(1/2)I
. However, since
[\nabla_{U}c_{i},U]=0
for any
i
, the functions
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1}
are Casimirs for any choice of
R_{0}
for
(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
.
choice of
r_{0}
for
(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
and
(\SL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
, the functions
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{n-1}
,
h_{11}
are Casimirs of
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
.

Remark 1.3
.

Let
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
be an aperiodic oriented BD pair and let
\mathcal{GC}^{D}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
be the generalized cluster structure on
D(\SL_{n})
(or on
D(\GL_{n})
) compatible with
\pi_{(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})}^{D}
(
\mathcal{GC}^{D}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{r},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{c})
was constructed in our previous work
[
31
]
). The initial seed for
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
was recognized as a subseed of some extended seed in
\mathcal{GC}^{D}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
. It is thus natural to ask whether the initial extended seed of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
can be obtained as a subseed of an extended seed from
\mathcal{GC}^{D}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{\Gamma})
. We verified in
n=3
and
n=4
for all BD triples
\mathbf{\Gamma}
that this is indeed the case. The corresponding mutation sequences are listed in the supplementary note
[
33
]
.

1.3
Main results

Let
G
be a connected reductive complex Lie group,
\mathfrak{g}
be its Lie algebra with a fixed decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{+}
and a set of simple roots
\Pi
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{-}
be the connected subgroups of
G
that correspond to
\mathfrak{n}_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{-}
. Decompose a generic element
U\in G
as
U=U_{\oplus}U_{-}
,
U_{\oplus}\in\mathcal{B}_{+}
,
U_{-}\in\mathcal{N}_{-}
. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}:=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
be a Belavin-Drinfeld triple. Define the rational map
\mathcal{Q}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
as

\mathcal{Q}(U):=\rho(U)^{-1}U\rho(U),\ \ \rho(U):=\prod_{i=1}^{\rightarrow}(\tilde{\gamma}^{*})^{i}(U_{-}),\ U\in G.

(1.27)

Proposition 1.4
.

If the
r_{0}
components of
\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger}
and
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
are the same, then the rational map
\mathcal{Q}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
is a Poisson isomorphism.

Section
3
is devoted to the proof of Proposition
1.4
. In Section
4.3
we specialize the group
G
to
G\in\{\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C}),\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})\}
and show that
\mathcal{Q}:(G,\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}))\dashrightarrow(G,\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}))
is a birational quasi-isomorphism. In Section
7.3
we construct the map
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}:(G,\mathcal{GC}_{g}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}))\dashrightarrow(G,\mathcal{GC}_{g}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}))
, which is a birational quasi-isomorphism in the
g
-convention. In Section
3.5
we prove a version of Proposition
1.4
for
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be any Belavin-Drinfeld triple of type
A_{n-1}
. Then the generalized cluster structure
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
has the following properties:

1)

The number of frozen variables is
k_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}+n
, and the exchange matrix has full rank;

2)

The generalized cluster structure
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
is complete; moreover, the initial extended cluster consists of irreducible elements of
\mathcal{O}(\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
that are coprime with their mutations;

3)

The global toric action of
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{k_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}+1}
upon
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
is induced by the action of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
upon
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
;

4)

For any choice of
r_{0}
,
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
is compatible with the Poisson bivector
\pi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
.

A similar theorem is valid in the case of
\SL_{n}
:

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be any Belavin-Drinfeld triple of type
A_{n-1}
. Then the generalized cluster structure
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
has the following properties:

1)

The number of frozen variables is
k_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}+(n-1)
, and the exchange matrix has full rank;

2)

The generalized cluster structure
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
is complete; moreover, the initial extended cluster consists of irreducible elements of
\mathcal{O}(\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
that are coprime with their mutations;

3)

The global toric action of
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{k_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}
upon
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
is induced by the action of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
upon
\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
;

4)

For any choice of
r_{0}
,
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
is compatible with the Poisson bivector
\pi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
.

We prove both theorems in Section
5
for
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
and in Section
7.4
for
\mathcal{GC}_{g}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
.

Given two BD triples
\mathbf{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
and
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}=(\tilde{\Gamma}_{1},\tilde{\Gamma}_{2},\theta)
of the same Lie type, let us write
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}
if
\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}\subsetneq\Gamma_{1}
,
\tilde{\Gamma}_{2}\subsetneq\Gamma_{2}
and
\gamma|_{\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}}=\theta
. If
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}
, then it is possible to choose the same
r_{0}
for both
\pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}^{\dagger}
and
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
.

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}
be two BD triples of type
A_{n-1}
and let
G\in\{\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C}),\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})\}
. Then there exists a birational quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{G}:(G,\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}))\dashrightarrow(G,\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}))
. Moreover,
\mathcal{G}:(G,\pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
is a Poisson isomorphism provided the
r_{0}
parts of
\pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}^{\dagger}
and
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
coincide.

If
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}
, then
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{Q}
in the
h
-convention and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}
in the
g
-convention. In Sections
4.4
-
4.5
we provide a more explicit formula for
\mathcal{G}
and prove the proposition for the
h
-convention. In the
g
-convention, the proof and the formulas are provided in Section
7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\Gamma_{2})
and
\mathcal{P}_{-}(\Gamma_{1})
be the parabolic subgroups of
\GL_{n}
generated by the BD data
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
. Define the subgroup
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{P}_{+}(\Gamma_{1})\times\mathcal{P}_{-}(\Gamma_{2})
via

\mathcal{D}:=\{(g^{\prime},g)\in\mathcal{P}_{+}(\Gamma_{1})\times\mathcal{P}_{-}(\Gamma_{2})\ |\ {\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(\mathring{\Pi}_{\Gamma_{1}}(g^{\prime}))=\mathring{\Pi}_{\Gamma_{2}}(g)\}

(1.28)

where
\mathring{\Pi}_{\Gamma_{1}}:\mathcal{P}_{+}(\Gamma_{1})\rightarrow\GL_{n}(\Gamma_{1})
and
\mathring{\Pi}_{\Gamma_{2}}:\mathcal{P}_{-}(\Gamma_{2})\rightarrow\GL_{n}(\Gamma_{2})
are the group projections upon the Levi factors of
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\Gamma_{1})
and
\mathcal{P}_{-}(\Gamma_{2})
, and the map
{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}
is a certain group lift of
\gamma
(see Section
4.1
for details). Define an action of
\mathcal{D}
upon
\GL_{n}
via

((g^{\prime},g),U)\mapsto g^{\prime}Ug^{-1},\ \ (g^{\prime},g)\in\mathcal{D},\ U\in\GL_{n}.

(1.29)

Proposition 1.8
.

Let
\psi
be a frozen variable in
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},G)
,
G\in\{\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C}),\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})\}
. Then the following statements hold:

1)

The variable
\psi
is semi-invariant with respect to the action (
1.29
);

2)

The variable
\psi
is log-canonical with any
u_{ij}
;

3)

The zero locus of
\psi
foliates into a union of symplectic leaves of
(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
.

In the
h
-convention, we prove Proposition
1.8
in Section
6.4
; in the
g
-convention, we prove it in Section
7.2
.

Remark 1.9
.

By default, we will always assume that the ground field is
\mathbb{C}
. However, all the above results hold for
\SL_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{R})
if one uses the same formula for the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
.

2
Background

In this section, we provide a necessary background on Poisson geometry, generalized cluster structures and birational quasi-isomorphisms. We will also use some well-known determinantal identities, which are listed in Appendix
C
.

2.1
Poisson geometry

In this section, we briefly recall relevant concepts from Poisson geometry. A more detailed account can be found in
[
5
]
,
[
8
]
and
[
30
]
.

Poisson-Lie groups.

A
Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
on a commutative algebra is a Lie bracket that satisfies the Leibniz rule. Given a manifold
M
, a
Poisson bivector field
on
M
is a section
\pi\in\Gamma(M,\bigwedge^{2}TM)
such that
\{f,g\}:=\pi(df\wedge dg)
is a Poisson bracket on the space of smooth functions on
M
. The pair
(M,\pi)
is called a
Poisson manifold
.

Definition 2.1
.

Given a Lie group
G
and a Poisson bivector field
\pi\in\Gamma(G,\Lambda^{2}TG)
, the pair
(G,\pi)
is called a
Poisson-Lie group
if

\pi_{gh}=[dL_{g}\otimes dL_{g}](\pi_{h})+[dR_{h}\otimes dR_{h}](\pi_{g}),\ \ g,h\in G,

(2.1)

where
L_{g}
and
R_{h}
are the left and right translations by
g
and
h
, respectively. Equivalently, if
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is the Poisson bracket defined by
\pi
, then the multiplication map from
G\times G
equipped with the direct product Poisson structure to
G
is Poisson.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the Lie algebra of
G
and
r\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
. If
G
is a connected Lie group, then the bivector field
3
3

3

If
G
is simple and complex, then any bivector field
\pi
that yields the structure of a Poisson-Lie group on
G
is of this form for some
r\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
.

\pi_{g}:=[dL_{g}\otimes dL_{g}](r)-[dR_{g}\otimes dR_{g}](r),\ \ g\in G,

(2.2)

defines the structure of a Poisson-Lie group on
G
if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

1)

The symmetric part of
r
is
\ad
-invariant;

2)

The
3
-tensor
[r,r]:=[r_{12},r_{13}]+[r_{12},r_{23}]+[r_{13},r_{23}]
is
\ad
-invariant, where
(a\otimes b)_{12}=a\otimes b\otimes 1
,
(a\otimes b)_{13}=a\otimes 1\otimes b
and
(a\otimes b)_{23}=1\otimes a\otimes b
,
a,b\in\mathfrak{g}
.

The
Classical Yang-Baxter equation (CYBE)
is the equation
[r,r]=0
. For simple complex Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
, Belavin and Drinfeld in
[
1
,
2
]
classified solutions of the CYBE that have a nondegenerate
\ad
-invariant symmetric part. The classification was partially extended by Hodges in
[
27
]
to the case of reductive complex Lie algebras (however, Hodges required the symmetric part of
r
to be a multiple of the Casimir element). A full classification of solutions of the CYBE with an arbitrary nondegenerate
\ad
-invariant symmetric part in the case of reductive complex Lie algebras was obtained by Delorme in
[
7
]
.

Extensions of the Belavin-Drinfeld maps.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a reductive complex Lie algebra,
\Pi
be a set of simple roots and
\langle\,,\,\rangle
be a nondegenerate invariant symmetric bilinear form on
\mathfrak{g}
.

Definition 2.2
.

A
Belavin-Drinfeld triple
(for conciseness, a
BD triple
) is a triple
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
with
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\subset\Pi
and
\gamma:\Gamma_{1}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2}
a nilpotent
4
4

4

The nilpotency condition means that for any
\alpha\in\Gamma_{1}
there exists a number
j
such that
\gamma^{j}(\alpha)\notin\Gamma_{1}
.
isometry.

Decompose
\mathfrak{g}
as
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{-}
, where
\mathfrak{n}_{+}=\bigoplus_{\alpha>0}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{-}=\bigoplus_{\alpha>0}\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}
are nilpotent subalgebras,
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
are root subspaces and
\mathfrak{h}
is a Cartan subalgebra. For every positive root
\alpha
, choose
e_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
and
e_{-\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}
such that
\langle e_{\alpha},e_{-\alpha}\rangle=1
, and set
h_{\alpha}:=[e_{\alpha},e_{-\alpha}]
. Let
\mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma_{2}}
be the semisimple Lie subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}
generated by
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
. Extend
\gamma
to an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\Gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{Z}\Gamma_{2}
, and then define
\gamma:\mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma_{1}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma_{2}}
via
\gamma(e_{\alpha})=e_{\gamma(\alpha)}
and
\gamma(h_{\alpha})=h_{\gamma(\alpha)}
. Let
\gamma^{*}:\mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma_{2}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma_{1}}
be the conjugate of
\gamma
with respect to the form on
\mathfrak{g}
. Extend both
\gamma
and
\gamma^{*}
by zero to
\mathfrak{g}
via setting
\gamma|_{\mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\perp}}=0
and
\gamma^{*}|_{\mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma_{2}}^{\perp}}=0
. Note that the extensions
\gamma,\gamma^{*}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
are still adjoint to one another.

The Belavin-Drinfeld classification.

Continuing in the setup of the previous paragraph, let
\pi_{>}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{n}_{+}
and
\pi_{<}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{n}_{-}
be the orthogonal projections, and let
\Omega_{>}
and
\Omega_{<}
be the
2
-tensors that correspond to
\pi_{>}
and
\pi_{<}
under the form on
\mathfrak{g}
. In addition, let
\Omega_{0}
be the restriction of the Casimir element
\Omega
to
\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathfrak{h}
. Consider an element
r_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathfrak{h}
that satisfies the following system of equations:

\displaystyle r_{0}+r_{0}^{t}=\Omega_{0};

(2.3)

\displaystyle[\gamma(\alpha)\otimes 1](r_{0})+[1\otimes\alpha](r_{0})=0,\ \ \alpha\in\Gamma_{1}

(2.4)

where
(a\otimes b)^{t}=b\otimes a
,
a,b\in\mathfrak{g}
.

Theorem 2.3

(Belavin & Drinfeld)
.

Under the above setup, if

r=r_{0}+\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right](\Omega_{>})-\left[1\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right](\Omega_{<})

(2.5)

where
r_{0}
is any solution of (
2.3
)-(
2.4
), then
r
satisfies CYBE. Moreover,

r+r^{t}=\Omega.

(2.6)

Conversely, if
r
is a solution of the CYBE such that
r
satisfies (
2.6
), then
r
assumes the form (
2.5
) for a suitable decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}
, for some Belavin-Drinfeld triple and some choice of the root vectors
e_{\alpha}
.

The element
r
from the theorem is called a
classical
r
-matrix
. If
\mathfrak{g}
is simple, then Theorem
2.3
provides a full classification of solutions
r\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
of the CYBE with nondegenerate
\ad
-invariant symmetric parts. Sometimes we will use the notation
r_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
along with
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
for the Poisson bivector on
G
defined by
r_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
via the formula (
2.2
).

Let us define
R_{+}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
and
R_{-}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
as linear maps via
\langle R_{+}(x),y\rangle=\langle r,x\otimes y\rangle
and
\langle R_{-}(x),y\rangle=-\langle r,y\otimes x\rangle
,
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
. Then
R_{+}-R_{-}=\id
and

R_{+}=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\pi_{>}-\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\pi_{<}+R_{0}\pi_{0}.

(2.7)

The system (
2.3
)-(
2.4
) can be rewritten in terms of a linear map
R_{0}:\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}
,
\langle R_{0}(x),y\rangle=\langle r_{0},x\otimes y\rangle
,
x,y\in\mathfrak{h}
, via

\displaystyle R_{0}+R_{0}^{*}=\id;

(2.8)

\displaystyle R_{0}(h_{\alpha}-\gamma(h_{\alpha}))=h_{\alpha},\ \ \alpha\in\Gamma_{1}

(2.9)

where
R_{0}^{*}
denotes the adjoint of
R_{0}
in
\mathfrak{h}
with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.

The Drinfeld Double.

Let
G
be a reductive connected complex Poisson-Lie group endowed with a nondegenerate symmetric invariant bilinear form on
\mathfrak{g}
and with a Poisson bivector field
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
defined by a classical
r
-matrix
r_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
relative some Belavin-Drinfeld triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Let
R_{+}
and
R_{-}
be defined from
r_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
as in the previous paragraph, and set
\mathfrak{d}:=\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
to be the direct sum of Lie algebras. Define a nondegenerate symmetric invariant bilinear form on
\mathfrak{d}
as

\langle(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\rangle=\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle-\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle,\ \ x_{1},\,x_{2},\,y_{1},\,y_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}.

(2.10)

As a vector space,
\mathfrak{d}
splits into the direct sum of the following isotropic Lie subalgebras:

\mathfrak{g}^{\delta}:=\{(x,x)\ |\ x\in\mathfrak{g}\},\ \ \mathfrak{g}^{*}:=\{(R_{+}(x),R_{-}(x))\ |\ x\in\mathfrak{g}\}

(2.11)

where
R_{+}
and
R_{-}
are defined from
r_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. Set
R^{\mathfrak{d}}_{+}:=P_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
where
P_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
is the projection of
\mathfrak{d}
onto
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, and let
r^{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathfrak{d}\otimes\mathfrak{d}
be the
2
-tensor that corresponds to
R^{\mathfrak{d}}_{+}
. Then
R_{+}^{\mathfrak{d}}
yields the structure of a Poisson-Lie group on the Lie group
D(G):=G\times G
via the Poisson bivector field

(\pi^{D}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})_{(g,h)}:=[dL_{(g,h)}\otimes dL_{(g,h)}](r^{\mathfrak{d}})-[dR_{(g,h)}\otimes dR_{(g,h)}](r^{\mathfrak{d}}),\ \ {(g,h)}\in D(G).

(2.12)

Definition 2.4
.

The Poisson-Lie group
(D(G),\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{D})
is called the
Drinfeld double
of
(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
.

The Poisson bracket on
D(G)
can be written in the form

\{f_{1},f_{2}\}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{D}=\langle R_{+}(E_{L}f_{1}),E_{L}f_{2}\rangle-\langle R_{+}(E_{R}f_{1}),E_{R}f_{2}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{X}^{R}f_{1},\nabla^{R}_{Y}f_{2}\rangle-\langle\nabla_{X}^{L}f_{1},\nabla_{Y}^{L}f_{2}\rangle

(2.13)

where
\nabla^{L}f_{i}=(\nabla_{X}^{L}f_{i},-\nabla_{Y}^{L}f_{i})
and
\nabla^{R}f_{i}=(\nabla_{X}^{R}f_{i},-\nabla_{Y}^{R}f_{i})
are the left and the right gradients, respectively,
E_{L}f_{i}=\nabla^{L}_{X}f_{i}+\nabla^{L}_{Y}f_{i}
and
E_{R}f_{i}=\nabla^{R}_{X}f_{i}+\nabla^{R}_{Y}f_{i}
. We define the gradients on
G
as
5
5

5

This convention is opposite to the one in
[
23
]
and
[
30
]
, but in this way the left gradient is the gradient in the left trivialization, and the right gradient is the gradient in the right trivialization of the group.

\langle\nabla^{L}f|_{g},x\rangle:=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}f(g\exp(tx)),\ \ \ \langle\nabla^{R}f|_{g},x\rangle:=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}f(\exp(tx)g),\ \ g\in G,\ x\in\mathfrak{g}.

(2.14)

The group
G
can be identified with the diagonal subgroup
G^{\delta}
of
D(G)
, which corresponds to the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\delta}
. The Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
on
G
can be expressed as

\{f_{1},f_{2}\}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}=\langle R_{+}(\nabla^{L}f_{1}),\nabla^{L}f_{2}\rangle-\langle R_{+}(\nabla^{R}f_{1}),\nabla^{R}f_{2}\rangle.

(2.15)

Additionally, the connected (immersed) Poisson-Lie subgroup
(G^{*},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{*})
of
D(G)
that corresponds to
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is called the
dual Poisson-Lie group of
G
.

The Poisson dual
G^{\dagger}
.

In the setup of the previous paragraph, consider the Drinfeld double
(D(G),\pi^{D}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
along with
(G^{*},\pi^{*}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
immersed into
D(G)
. Consider the map

D(G)\ni(g,h)\mapsto g^{-1}h\in G.

(2.16)

The pushforward of
\pi^{D}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
under the above map is denoted as
\pi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
, and the image of
G^{*}
is an open subset of
G
denoted as
G^{\dagger}
. The Poisson bivector
\pi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is given by

\begin{split}(\pi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})_{g}=\left[dL_{g}\otimes dL_{g}-dR_{g}\right.&\left.\otimes dL_{g}-dL_{g}\otimes dR_{g}+dR_{g}\otimes dR_{g}\right](r)-\\
-&\left[dL_{g}\otimes dL_{g}-dR_{g}\otimes dL_{g}\right](\Omega),\ \ g\in G,\end{split}

(2.17)

and the corresponding Poisson bracket is

\{f_{1},f_{2}\}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}=\langle R_{+}(\nabla^{L}f_{1}-\nabla^{R}f_{1}),\nabla^{L}f_{2}-\nabla^{R}f_{2}\rangle-\langle\nabla^{L}f_{1}-\nabla^{R}f_{1},\nabla^{L}f_{2}\rangle.

(2.18)

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a trivial BD triple (that is,
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset
), we will denote the corresponding Poisson bivector as
\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger}
and the Poisson bracket as
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\text{std}}^{\dagger}
.

Symplectic foliation and Poisson submanifolds.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold. An immersed submanifold
S\subseteq M
is called a
Poisson submanifold
if
\pi|_{S}\in\Gamma(S,\bigwedge^{2}TS)
. Define a morphism of vector bundles
\pi^{\#}:TM^{*}\rightarrow TM
via
\langle\pi^{\#}(\xi),\eta\rangle:=\langle\pi,\xi\wedge\eta\rangle
,
\xi,\eta\in T_{p}^{*}M
,
p\in M
.
The Poisson bivector
\pi
is called
nondegenerate
if
\pi^{\#}
is an isomorphism of vector bundles. A
symplectic leaf
is a maximal (by inclusion) connected Poisson submanifold
S
of
M
for which
\pi|_{S}
is nondegenerate. It is a theorem that any Poisson manifold
M
is a union of its symplectic leaves.

In this paper, we will be interested in symplectic leaves of the zero loci of frozen variables (see Proposition
1.8
). Assume that
M
is a smooth real or complex affine variety. Let us recall that an ideal
J\subseteq\mathcal{O}(M)
is called a
Poisson ideal
if
\{\mathcal{O}(M),J\}\subseteq J
. The following proposition is a restatement of
[
29
, Remark 2.4]
in the setup of the current paper:

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{O}(M)
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

p
is a prime element of
\mathcal{O}(M)
and the principal ideal
(p)
is a Poisson ideal;

(ii)

The zero locus of
p
is a union of symplectic leaves of
(M,\pi)
.

Proposition
2.5
can also be generalized to the case of not necessarily smooth affine varieties; see
[
25
, Proposition 2.3]
.

2.2
Generalized cluster algebras

In this subsection, we briefly review the relevant definitions and propositions from the theory of generalized cluster structures. An expanded account with proofs can be found in
[
20
]
. The concept of a generalized cluster algebra is based on an earlier definition given in
[
6
]
(see
[
20
, Remark 2.1]
). Throughout the subsection, we let
\mathcal{F}
to be a field of rational functions in
N+M
independent variables with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}
. We refer to
\mathcal{F}
as the
ambient field
.

Definition 2.6

(Seeds)
.

An
extended seed
is a triple
\Sigma:=(\mathbf{x},B,\mathcal{P})
that consists of the following data:

1)

A collection of elements
\mathbf{x}:=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N+M})
of
\mathcal{F}
such that
\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{Q}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N+M})
(an
extended cluster
). The elements
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N}
are called
cluster variables
and the elements
x_{N+1},\ldots,x_{N+M}
are called
frozen variables
;

2)

An integer matrix
B:=(b_{ij}\ |\ i\in[1,N],\ j\in[1,N+M])
such that the principal part
B^{[1,N]}
is skew-symmetrizable
6
6

6

Recall that a square matrix
A
of size
N\times N
is
skew-symmetrizable
if there exists a diagonal matrix
D
with positive entries such that
DA
is skew-symmetric.
(an
exchange matrix
);

3)

For each
i\in[1,N]
, set
d_{i}
to be a factor of
\gcd(b_{ij}\ |\ j\in[1,N])
(the
multiplicity
of
x_{i}
). For each
i\in[1,N]
, define the
i
th string

p_{i}:=(p_{ir})_{r\in[0,d_{i}]}
to be a collection of monomials
p_{ir}
in the frozen variables such that
p_{i0}=p_{id_{i}}=1
. The collection of
strings

\mathcal{P}
is given by
\mathcal{P}:=\{p_{i}\}_{i\in[1,N]}
.

Definition 2.7

(
\tau
-monomials.)
.

Let
\Sigma:=(\mathbf{x},B,\mathcal{P})
be an extended seed. For
k\in[1,N]
, the
cluster
\tau
-monomials

u_{k;>}
and
u_{k;<}
are monomials in the cluster variables of
\Sigma
given by

u_{k;>}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N,\\
b_{ki}>0\end{subarray}}x_{i}^{b_{ki}/d_{k}},\ \ u_{k;<}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N,\\
b_{ki}<0\end{subarray}}x_{i}^{-b_{ki}/d_{k}}.

(2.19)

For
k\in[1,N]
and
r\in[0,d_{k}]
, the
stable
\tau
-monomials

v_{k;>}^{[r]}
and
v_{k;<}^{[r]}
are monomials in the frozen variables given by

v_{k;>}^{[r]}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}N+1\leq i\leq N+M,\\
b_{ki}>0\end{subarray}}x_{i}^{\lfloor rb_{ki}/d_{k}\rfloor},\ \ v_{k;<}^{[r]}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}N+1\leq i\leq N+M,\\
b_{ki}<0\end{subarray}}x_{i}^{\lfloor-rb_{ki}/d_{k}\rfloor},

(2.20)

where the product over an empty set by definition equals
1
and
\lfloor m\rfloor
denotes the floor of
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 2.8

(Generalized cluster mutations)
.

Let
\Sigma:=(\mathbf{x},B,\mathcal{P})
be an extended seed. For
k\in[1,N]
, a
generalized cluster mutation in direction
k
of
\Sigma
is an extended seed
\Sigma^{\prime}:=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},B^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
defined as follows.

1)

Let
x_{k}^{\prime}
be an element given by the
generalized exchange relation
:

x_{k}x_{k}^{\prime}:=\sum_{r=0}^{d_{k}}p_{kr}u_{k;>}^{r}v_{k;>}^{[r]}u_{k;<}^{d_{k}-r}v_{k;<}^{[d_{k}-r]}

(2.21)

where
u_{k;>}
and
u_{k;<}
are the cluster
\tau
-monomials and
v_{k;<}
and
v_{k;<}
are the stable
\tau
-monomials constructed relative
\Sigma
.
The extended cluster
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is given by
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}:=(\mathbf{x}\setminus\{x_{k}\})\cup\{x_{k}^{\prime}\}
.

2)

The exchange matrix
B^{\prime}:=(b_{ij}^{\prime}\ |\ i\in[1,N],\ j\in[1,N+M])
is given by

b_{ij}^{\prime}:=\begin{cases}-b_{ij}\ \ &\text{if }i=k\text{ or }j=k;\\
b_{ij}+\dfrac{|b_{ik}|b_{kj}+b_{ik}|b_{kj}|}{2}\ \ &\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(2.22)

3)

Define
p_{k}^{\prime}:=(p_{kr})_{r\in[0,d_{k}]}
as
p_{kr}:=p_{k,d_{k}-r}
,
r\in[0,d_{k}]
. The collection of strings
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is given by
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}:=(\mathcal{P}\setminus\{p_{k}\})\cup\{p_{k}^{\prime}\}
.

The extended seeds
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are also called
adjacent
.

Let us make a few remarks on the above definition:

1)

A frozen variable
x_{i}
is called
isolated
if
b_{ji}=0
for all
j\in[1,N]
. The definition of
b_{ij}^{\prime}
implies that if
x_{i}
is isolated in
\Sigma
, it is isolated in
\Sigma^{\prime}
as well;

2)

Since
\gcd\{b_{ij}\ |\ 1\leq j\leq N\}=\gcd\{b_{ij}^{\prime}\ |\ 1\leq j\leq N\}
, the numbers
d_{1},\ldots,d_{N}
are the same in
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
;

3)

If the string
p_{k}
is trivial, then the generalized exchange relation (
2.21
) becomes the exchange relation from the theory of cluster algebras of geometric type:

x_{k}x_{k}^{\prime}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N+M\\
b_{ki>0}\end{subarray}}x_{i}^{b_{ki}}+\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N+M\\
b_{ki<0}\end{subarray}}x_{i}^{-b_{ki}}.

(2.23)

In fact, the generalized cluster structures studied in this paper have only one nontrivial string, so all the exchange relations except one are given by (
2.23
).

4)

For each
i\in[1,N]
, set
v_{i;>}:=v_{i;>}^{[d_{i}]}
,
v_{i;<}:=v_{i;<}^{[d_{i}]}
; define

q_{ir}:=\frac{v_{i;>}^{r}v_{i;<}^{d_{i}-r}}{(v_{i;>}^{[r]}v_{i;<}^{[d_{i}-r]})^{d_{i}}},\ \ \hat{p}_{ir}:=\frac{p_{ir}^{d_{i}}}{q_{ir}},\ \ i\in[1,N],\ r\in[0,d_{i}].

Then the relation (
2.21
) can be rewritten as

x_{k}x_{k}^{\prime}=\sum_{r=0}^{d_{k}}(\hat{p}_{kr}v_{k;>}^{r}v_{k;<}^{d_{k}-r})^{1/d_{k}}u_{k;>}^{r}u_{k;<}^{d_{k}-r}.

(2.24)

The expression
(\hat{p}_{kr}v_{k;>}^{r}v_{k;<}^{d_{k}-r})^{1/d_{k}}
is a monomial in the frozen variables. Note also that the mutation rule for
\hat{p}_{ir}
is the same as for
p_{ir}
(see Part
3
of Definition
2.8
).

Definition 2.9

(Generalized cluster structures)
.

Two extended seeds
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are called
mutation equivalent
if there’s a sequence of extended seeds
\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{m}
where
\Sigma_{1}:=\Sigma
and
\Sigma_{m}:=\Sigma^{\prime}
, such that
\Sigma_{i+1}
and
\Sigma_{i}
are adjacent for each
i
. For a fixed extended seed
\Sigma
, the set of all extended seeds that are mutation equivalent to
\Sigma
is called a
generalized cluster structure
and is denoted as
\mathcal{GC}(\Sigma)
or simply as
\mathcal{GC}
. The
rank
of
\mathcal{GC}
is the number
N
.

In practice, we fix one extended seed
\Sigma_{0}:=(\mathbf{x}_{0},B_{0},\mathcal{P}_{0})
and provide its full description. In this case, we call
\Sigma_{0}
the
initial extended seed
(correspondingly,
\mathbf{x}_{0}
is the
initial extended cluster
and
B_{0}
is the
initial exchange matrix
). Also, when several generalized cluster structures are considered at once, we will denote by
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{GC})
the ambient field of
\mathcal{GC}
.

Algebras associated to
\mathcal{GC}
.

Let
\mathcal{GC}
be a generalized cluster structure and
x_{N+1},\ldots,x_{N+M}
be its set of frozen variables. Define the rings
\mathbb{A}:=\mathbb{Z}[x_{N+1},\ldots,x_{N+M}]
and
\bar{\mathbb{A}}:=\mathbb{Z}[x_{N+1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{N+M}^{\pm 1}]
.

Definition 2.10
.

A
ground ring

\hat{\mathbb{A}}
is a subring of
\bar{\mathbb{A}}
that contains
\mathbb{A}
.

Definition 2.11
.

The
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{GC})
generated by all the cluster variables in
\mathcal{GC}
is called the
generalized cluster algebra
.

For a fixed ground ring
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
and an extended cluster
\mathbf{x}:=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N+M})
in
\mathcal{GC}
, define the
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
-algebras

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{x}):=\hat{\mathbb{A}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}],\ \ \ \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}):=\hat{\mathbb{A}}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{N}^{\pm 1}].

(2.25)

For an extended seed
\Sigma
that contains the extended cluster
\mathbf{x}
, we will also write
\mathcal{L}(\Sigma):=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x})
and
\mathcal{A}(\Sigma):=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{x})
.

Definition 2.12
.

The
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
-algebra given by

\bar{\mathcal{A}}:=\bar{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{GC}):=\bigcap_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{GC}}\mathcal{L}(\Sigma)

(2.26)

is called the
generalized upper cluster algebra
.

One of the central results of the theory is the
generalized Laurent phenomenon
, which states that there is an inclusion
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{A}}
. For cluster algebras, the result was first proved in
[
13
, Theorem 3.1]
; later, it was extended to generalized cluster algebras in
[
6
, Theorem 2.5]
.

Upper bounds.

We continue in the setup of the previous paragraph. Let
\mathbb{T}_{N}
be a labeled
N
-regular tree. Associate with each vertex an extended seed so that adjacent extended seeds are adjacent in the tree, and if an extended seed
\Sigma^{\prime}
is adjacent to
\Sigma
in direction
k
, label the corresponding edge in the tree with number
k
. A
nerve

\mathcal{N}
in
\mathbb{T}_{N}
is a subtree on
N+1
vertices such that all its edges have different labels (for instance, a star is a nerve).

Definition 2.13
.

An
upper bound

\bar{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{N})
is the
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
-algebra defined as

\bar{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{N}):=\bigcap_{\Sigma\in V(\mathcal{N})}\mathcal{L}(\Sigma)

(2.27)

where
V(\mathcal{N})
stands for the vertex set of
\mathcal{N}
.

Upper bounds were first defined and studied in
[
3
]
. Let
L
be the number of isolated frozen variables in
\mathcal{GC}
. For the
i
th nontrivial string in
\mathcal{P}
, let
{B}(i)
be a
(d_{i}-1)\times L
matrix such that the
r
th row consists of the exponents of the isolated variables in
p_{ir}
. The following result was proved in
[
20
, Theorem 3.11]
:

Proposition 2.14
.

Assume that the extended exchange matrix has full rank and let
\rank{B}(i)=d_{i}-1
for any nontrivial string in
\mathcal{P}
. Then for any upper bound
\mathcal{N}
in
\mathcal{GC}
,
\bar{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{N})=\bar{\mathcal{A}}
.

Poisson structures in
\mathcal{GC}
.

Let
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
be a Poisson bracket on
\mathcal{F}
and let
{\mathbf{x}}
be any extended cluster in
\mathcal{GC}
. We say that
{\mathbf{x}}
is
log-canonical
if for each
i,j\in[1,N+M]
,
\{x_{i},x_{j}\}=\omega_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}
, where
\omega_{ij}\in\mathbb{Q}
. The matrix
\Omega:=(\omega_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{N+M}
is called the
coefficient matrix
of the Poisson bracket with respect to the extended cluster
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
.

Definition 2.15
.

The generalized cluster structure
\mathcal{GC}
is called
compatible
with a Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
if each extended cluster in
\mathcal{GC}
is log-canonical.

The following result is the key connection between cluster algebras and Poisson geometry. Given a log-canonical collection of elements, it shows how to reconstruct the exchange matrix
B
from the coefficient matrix
\Omega
. Its earliest version was proved in
[
17
, Theorem 1.4]
.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},B,\mathcal{P})\in\mathcal{GC}
be an extended seed that satisfies the following properties:

1)

The extended cluster
{\mathbf{x}}
is log-canonical with respect to the Poisson bracket;

2)

For a diagonal matrix with positive entries
D
such that
DB^{[1,N]}
is skew-symmetric, there exists a diagonal
N\times N
matrix
\Delta
such that
B\Omega=\begin{bmatrix}\Delta&0\end{bmatrix}
and such that
D\Delta
is a multiple of the identity matrix;

3)

The Laurent polynomials
\hat{p}_{ir}
are Casimirs of the Poisson bracket.

Then any other extended seed in
\mathcal{GC}
satisfies properties
1
,
2
(with the same
\Delta
) and
3
. In particular,
\mathcal{GC}
is compatible with
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
.

Condition
2
has the following interpretation, which is used in practice. For each
i\in[1,N]
, define the
y
-variable
of the cluster variable
x_{i}
as
y_{i}:=y(x_{i}):=\prod_{j=1}^{N+M}x_{j}^{b_{ij}}
. Then Condition
2
is equivalent to
\{\log y_{i},\log x_{j}\}=\delta_{ij}\Delta_{ii}
, where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker symbol. Note also that Condition
2
implies that
B
has full rank.

\mathcal{GC}
on varieties.

Let
V
be a complex affine variety,
\mathcal{O}(V)
be its coordinate ring and
\mathbb{C}(V)
be its field of rational functions. For an extended cluster
\mathbf{x}
in
\mathcal{GC}
, set

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{x})\otimes\mathbb{C},\

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x})\otimes\mathbb{C};

(2.28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{GC}):=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{GC})\otimes\mathbb{C},\

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{GC}):=\bar{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{GC})\otimes\mathbb{C};

(2.29)

For the ambient field
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathcal{GC}
, we also set
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{C}}:=\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 2.17
.

The pair
(V,\mathcal{GC})
is a
generalized cluster structure on the affine variety
V
if there is a fixed embedding
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{x}_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(V)
. A variable
x
in
\mathcal{GC}
is called
regular
if
x\in\mathcal{O}(V)
; an extended cluster
\mathbf{x}
is called
regular
if for each
x\in\mathbf{x}
,
x
is regular; the generalized cluster structure
(V,\mathcal{GC})
is called
regular
if
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{GC})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(V)
. Finally,
(V,\mathcal{GC})
is called
complete
if
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{GC})=\mathcal{O}(V)
.

Toric actions.

Let
\mathbf{x}:=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N+M})
be an extended cluster in some generalized cluster structure
\mathcal{GC}
, as above. Given an integer matrix
W:=(w_{ij}\ |\ i\in[1,N+M],\ j\in[1,s])
, consider an action of
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{s}
upon
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{C}}
by field automorphisms: for every
\mathbf{t}:=(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{s})\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{s}
, the corresponding field automorphism
\sigma_{\mathbf{t}}
is given by

\sigma_{\mathbf{t}}(x_{i})=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{s}t_{j}^{w_{ij}}\right)\cdot x_{i},\ \ i\in[1,N+M].

(2.30)

Definition 2.18
.

A
local toric action
of rank
s
upon an extended cluster
\mathbf{x}
is the action by field automorphisms given by (
2.30
) such that
W
is of rank
s
. It is called a
global toric action
if the action is equivariant
7
7

7

In other words, given any other extended cluster
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
mutation equivalent to
\mathbf{x}
, there exists a matrix
W^{\prime}
such that the action is still of the form (
2.30
).
with respect to mutations.

The first version of following proposition was proved in
[
17
, Lemma 2.3]
for cluster algebras of geometric type, and it was later extended to generalized cluster algebras in
[
20
, Proposition 2.6]
.

Proposition 2.19
.

A local toric action with a weight matrix
W
is a global toric action if
\tilde{B}W=0
and the Laurent polynomials
\hat{p}_{ir}
are invariant with respect to the action.

When all the strings are trivial, the statement of Proposition
2.19
is
if and only if
.

Quivers.

A
quiver
is a directed multigraph with no
1
- and
2
-cycles. Pick an extended seed
\Sigma:=({\mathbf{x}},{B},\mathcal{P})
and let
D:=\diag(d_{1}^{-1},\ldots,d_{N}^{-1})
be a diagonal matrix with
d_{i}
’s defined as above. Assume that
DB^{[1,N]}
is skew-symmetric. Then the matrix

\hat{B}:=\begin{bmatrix}DB^{[1,N]}&{B}^{[N+1,N+M]}\\
-({B}^{[N+1,N+M]})^{T}&0\end{bmatrix}

(2.31)

is the adjacency matrix of a quiver
Q
, in which each vertex
i
corresponds to a variable
x_{i}\in{\mathbf{x}}
. The vertices that correspond to cluster variables are called
mutable
, the vertices that correspond to frozen variables are called
frozen
, and the vertices that correspond to isolated variables are called
isolated
. For each
i
, the number
d_{i}
is called the
multiplicity
of the
i
th vertex. If one mutates the extended seed
\Sigma
, then the quiver of the resulting extended seed can be obtained from
Q
via the following steps:

1)

For each path
i\rightarrow k\rightarrow j
, add an arrow
i\rightarrow j
;

2)

If there is a pair of arrows
i\rightarrow j
and
j\rightarrow i
, remove both;

3)

Flip the orientation of all arrows going in and out of the vertex
k
.

The above process is called a
quiver mutation in direction
k
(or
at vertex
k
). Instead of describing the exchange matrix
{B}
, we describe the corresponding quiver and multiplicities
d_{i}
’s.

2.3
Birational quasi-isomorphisms

In this subsection, we state some of the results on birational quasi-isomorphisms from
[
32
]
that are used in the proofs of Theorems
1.5
and
1.6
. The statements are adjusted for generalized cluster structures in the coordinate rings of complex affine varieties.
We assume that for all generalized cluster structures, the ground rings are chosen so that
\hat{\mathbb{A}}=\mathbb{A}
(in other words, we do not localize at the frozen variables).

Markers and related triples.

Let
\mathcal{GC}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
be generalized cluster structures of ranks
N
and
\tilde{N}
with
M
and
\tilde{M}
frozen variables, respectively. Assume that
\tilde{M}-M>0
and
M+N=\tilde{M}+\tilde{N}
. Let
\fr_{\mathcal{GC}}
and
\fr_{\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}}
be the indices of the frozen variables in
\mathcal{GC}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
. For a bijection
\kappa:[1,N+M]\rightarrow[1,N+M]
, set

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{I}}(\kappa):=\fr_{\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}}\setminus\kappa(\fr_{\mathcal{GC}}),

(2.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\kappa):=\kappa^{-1}(\tilde{\mathcal{I}}(\kappa)).

(2.33)

Denote by
d_{i}
and
\tilde{d}_{j}
the multiplicities of the variables with indices
i
and
j
in
\mathcal{GC}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
, respectively.

Definition 2.20
.

A
marker

\kappa
for the pair
(\mathcal{GC},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})
is a bijection
k:[1,N+M]\rightarrow[1,N+M]
such that
\kappa(\fr_{\mathcal{GC}})\subset\fr_{\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}}
and for any
i\in[1,N]\setminus\mathcal{I}(\kappa)
,
d_{i}=\tilde{d}_{\kappa(i)}
. For a given marker
\kappa
, the triple
(\mathcal{GC},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}},\kappa)
is called a
related
triple. The sets
\mathcal{I}(\kappa)
and
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}(\kappa)
are called the sets of
marked indices
in
\mathcal{GC}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
, respectively.

Assume that
(\mathcal{GC},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}},\kappa)
is a related triple. Fix initial extended clusters
\mathbf{x}_{0}:=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N+M})
and
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0}:=(\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\ldots,\tilde{x}_{N+M})
in
\mathcal{GC}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
, respectively. Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{0}(\kappa):=\{x_{i}\in\mathbf{x}_{0}\ |\ i\in\mathcal{I}(\kappa)\},

(2.34)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0}(\kappa):=\{\tilde{x}_{i}\in\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0}\ |\ i\in\tilde{\mathcal{I}}(\kappa)\}.

(2.35)

Definition 2.21
.

The elements of
\mathbf{x}_{0}(\kappa)
and
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0}(\kappa)
are called the
marked variables
in
\mathcal{GC}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
.

Definition 2.22
.

An extended cluster
\mathbf{x}
in
\mathcal{GC}
is called
marked
if
\mathbf{x}
is obtained by mutating
\mathbf{x}_{0}
along the sequence

x_{i_{1}}\rightarrow x_{i_{2}}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow x_{i_{m}}

(2.36)

such that
i_{j}\notin\mathcal{I}(\kappa)
for all
j\in[1,m]
.

In particular,
\mathbf{x}_{0}
itself is an extended marked cluster. A marker
\kappa
induces a natural bijection
8
8

8

To be more precise, we label extended marked clusters in
\mathcal{GC}
and extended clusters in
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
by paths (
2.36
).
between extended marked clusters in
\mathcal{GC}
and all extended clusters in
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
, as follows. If
\mathbf{x}
is obtained from
\mathbf{x}_{0}
via the sequence (
2.36
), we set
\kappa(\mathbf{x})
to be an extended cluster in
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
obtained from
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
via the sequence

\tilde{x}_{\kappa(i_{1})}\rightarrow\tilde{x}_{\kappa(i_{2})}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\tilde{x}_{\kappa(i_{m})}.

(2.37)

Definition 2.23
.

The extended clusters
\mathbf{x}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}:=\kappa(\mathbf{x})
are called
related
via
\kappa
. For any
i\in[1,N+M]
, the variables
x_{i}\in\mathbf{x}
and
\tilde{x}_{\kappa(i)}\in\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
are called
related
, and if
i\in[1,N]\setminus\mathcal{I}(\kappa)
, the corresponding
y
-variables
y_{i}:=y(x_{i})
and
\tilde{y}_{\kappa(i)}:=y(\tilde{x}_{\kappa(i)})
are called
related
.

By default, if
x
denotes a variable from an extended cluster in
\mathcal{GC}
,
\tilde{x}
denotes the corresponding related variable in
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
.

Quasi-isomorphisms

Here, we introduce the notion of a quasi-isomorphism suitable for our purposes
9
9

9

As we explained in
[
32
]
, the closest notion in literature to our version is the one defined by C. Fraser in
[
11
]
. The difference is, C. Fraser assumes
N=\tilde{N}
and arbitrary
M
and
\tilde{M}
, whereas we insist upon
N+M=\tilde{N}+\tilde{M}
and
\tilde{M}-M>0
.
along with some basic results. We let
(\mathcal{GC},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}},\kappa)
to be a related triple of generalized cluster structures with fixed initial extended clusters.

Definition 2.24
.

A
quasi-isomorphism

\mathcal{Q}:\mathcal{GC}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
is a field isomorphism
\mathcal{Q}:\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{GC})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})
such that the following conditions hold:

1)

For any pair
(\mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}})
of related extended clusters, there exists an integer matrix

\Lambda(\mathbf{x}):=\left(\lambda_{ij}\ |\ i\in[1,N+M],\ j\in{\mathcal{I}}(\kappa)\right)

(2.38)

such that
\lambda_{ii}=1
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}(\kappa)
and

\mathcal{Q}(x_{i})=\tilde{x}_{\kappa(i)}\prod_{j\in{\mathcal{I}}(\kappa)\setminus\{i\}}\tilde{x}_{\kappa(j)}^{\lambda_{ij}}

(2.39)

for each
i\in[1,N+M]
.

2)

For every
i\in[1,N]\setminus\mathcal{I}(\kappa)
, every pair
(x_{i},\tilde{x}_{\kappa(i)})
of related variables and the corresponding hatted strings
\{\hat{p}_{ir}\}_{r=0}^{d_{i}}
and
\{\tilde{\hat{p}}_{ir}\}_{r=0}^{d_{i}}
,
\mathcal{Q}(\hat{p}_{ir})=\tilde{\hat{p}}_{ir}
,
r\in[0,d_{i}]
.

Note that the above definition is not symmetric: we do not consider
\mathcal{Q}^{-1}
as a quasi-isomorphism.

Let
\tilde{B}
and
B
denote the initial exchange matrices in
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathcal{C}
, and let
\tilde{Q}
be the initial quiver in
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
. If
i,j\in[1,\tilde{N}]
, set

i\sim j\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ i\text{ and }j\text{ can be connected by a path consisting of mutable vertices in }\tilde{Q}.

(2.40)

Denote by
[i]
the equivalence class of
i\in[1,\tilde{N}]
. For integer matrices
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
of the size
\tilde{N}\times[1,\tilde{N}+\tilde{M}]
, define an equivalence relation
10
10

10

Let
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
be the quivers associated with
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
. Remove all the frozen variables from
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
. Then up to flipping the orientation in each connected component of the resulting quivers,
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
coincide.
:

B_{1}\sim B_{2}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \forall i\in[1,\tilde{N}]:\ [B_{1}]_{[i]}=\pm[B_{2}]_{[i]}.

(2.41)

Proposition 2.25
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}:\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{GC})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})
be a field isomorphism with the following properties:

1)

There exists an integer matrix
\Lambda:=\left(\lambda_{ij}\ |\ i\in[1,N+M],j\in{\mathcal{I}}(\kappa)\right)
such that
\lambda_{ii}=1
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}(\kappa)
and such that (
2.39
) holds for each
x_{i}\in\mathbf{x}_{0}
;

2)

For every
i\in[1,N]\setminus\mathcal{I}(\kappa)
, every pair
(x_{i},\tilde{x}_{\kappa(i)})
of related variables and the corresponding hatted strings
\{\hat{p}_{ir}\}_{r=0}^{d_{i}}
and
\{\tilde{\hat{p}}_{ir}\}_{r=0}^{d_{i}}
,
\mathcal{Q}(\hat{p}_{ir})=\tilde{\hat{p}}_{ir}
,
r\in[0,d_{i}]
.

Then
\mathcal{Q}
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if

3)

For each
y
-variable
y_{i}
in
\mathbf{x}_{0}
,
i\notin\mathcal{I}(\kappa)
,
\mathcal{Q}(y_{i})=\tilde{y}_{\kappa(i)}

where
\tilde{B}
is considered up to the equivalence (
2.41
).

Let us introduce the following notation: if
A
is an
n_{1}\times n_{2}
matrix,
n_{1},n_{2}\leq N+M
, then
\kappa^{-1}(A)
is an
n_{1}\times n_{2}
matrix such that
(\kappa^{-1}(A))_{ij}=A_{\kappa(i),\kappa(j)}
. If
B
is another matrix of size
n_{2}\times n_{3}
,
n_{3}\leq N+M
, then
\kappa^{-1}(AB)=\kappa^{-1}(A)\kappa^{-1}(B)
. In addition, let us extend
\kappa
to a field isomorphism
\kappa:\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{GC})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})
via
\kappa(x_{i})=\tilde{x}_{\kappa(i)}
in the initial extended clusters. The next proposition establishes a connection between toric actions and quasi-isomorphisms.

Proposition 2.26
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}(\kappa)=\{m\}
for some
m\in[1,N]
and that
\mathcal{GC}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
admit global toric actions of rank
1
. Let
\omega
and
\tilde{\omega}
be the weight vectors of the toric actions in the initial extended clusters. Assume the following conditions:

1)

For every
i\in[1,\tilde{N}]
and
r\in[0,d_{i}]
, the Laurent monomials
\hat{p}_{ir}
and
\tilde{\hat{p}}_{ir}
are invariant with respect to the toric actions and
\kappa(\hat{p}_{ir})=\tilde{\hat{p}}_{\kappa(i)r}
;

2)

For each
i\in[1,N+M]
,
(\omega_{i}-\tilde{\omega}_{\kappa(i)})\tilde{\omega}_{\kappa(m)}^{-1}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
w_{m}=\tilde{w}_{\kappa(m)}
;

3)

The following equality holds:

[\kappa^{-1}(\tilde{B})]_{[1,N]\setminus\{m\}}^{[1,N+M]\setminus\{m\}}=B^{[1,N+M]\setminus\{m\}}_{[1,N]\setminus\{m\}}.

(2.42)

Set

\Lambda_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{0}):=\Lambda_{i}:=\begin{cases}\dfrac{w_{i}-\tilde{w}_{\kappa(i)}}{\tilde{w}_{\kappa(m)}}\ &\text{if}\ i\neq m\\[3.0pt]
1\ &\text{if}\ i=m.\end{cases}

(2.43)

Then
\Lambda
defines a quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{Q}:\mathcal{GC}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
. Moreover, if
(\mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}})
is a pair of related extended clusters and
w^{\prime}
and
\tilde{w}^{\prime}
are the corresponding weight vectors of the toric actions, then the corresponding matrix
\Lambda(\mathbf{x})
is given by

\Lambda_{i}(\mathbf{x}):=\begin{cases}\dfrac{w_{i}^{\prime}-\tilde{w}_{\kappa(i)}^{\prime}}{\tilde{w}_{\kappa(m)}}\ &\text{if}\ i\neq m\\[3.0pt]
1\ &\text{if}\ i=m.\end{cases}

(2.44)

An example of a quasi-isomorphism that arises from toric actions is given in Section
4.5
. For other examples, see
[
31
, Proposition 5.3]
.

Birational quasi-isomorphisms.

In addition to the setup of the previous paragraph, we now assume that both
\mathcal{GC}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}
are generalized cluster structures on some complex affine varieties
V
and
\widetilde{V}
, respectively. Here, we introduce the notion of a birational quasi-isomorphism.

Definition 2.27
.

A birational map
\mathcal{Q}:\widetilde{V}\dashrightarrow V
is called a
birational quasi-isomorphism
if the following conditions hold:

1)

The map
\mathcal{Q}
induces an isomorphism of the
\mathbb{C}
-algebras:
11
11

11

By
\mathbf{x}_{0}(\kappa)^{\pm 1}
we mean the localization at each
x_{i}\in\mathbf{x}_{0}(\kappa)
.
