\mathcal{Q}^{*}:\mathcal{O}(V)[\mathbf{x}_{0}(\kappa)^{\pm 1}]\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}(\widetilde{V})[\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0}(\kappa)^{\pm 1}];

(2.45)

2)

The field isomorphism
\mathcal{Q}^{*}:\mathbb{C}(V)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}(\widetilde{V})
restricts to a quasi-isomorphism

\mathcal{Q}^{*}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{GC})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}}).

For a subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{C}(V)
, let us define the following predicates:

\displaystyle\Irr(A):=A\subseteq\mathcal{O}(V)\ \text{and}\ \forall a\in A:\,a\text{ is irreducible;}

(2.46)

\displaystyle\Cop(A):=A\subseteq\mathcal{O}(V)\ \text{and}\ \text{all elements in }A\text{ are pairwise coprime}.

(2.47)

Likewise, we define
\Irr(\tilde{A})
and
\Cop(\tilde{A})
for
\tilde{A}\subseteq\mathbb{C}(\widetilde{V})
by augmenting all the entries in (
2.46
)–(
2.47
) with a tilde. For
i\in[1,N]
, denote by
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\prime}
the mutation of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
in direction
i
.

Proposition 2.28
.

Let
V
and
\widetilde{V}
be complex affine varieties such that the coordinate rings
\mathcal{O}(V)
and
\mathcal{O}(\widetilde{V})
are factorial
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, and such that
\mathcal{O}(V)^{*}=\mathcal{O}(\widetilde{V})^{*}=\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Let
(V,\mathcal{GC})
and
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})
be generalized cluster structures on the varieties with fixed initial extended seeds
(\mathbf{x}_{0},B,\mathcal{P})
and
(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\tilde{B},\tilde{\mathcal{P}})
. Assume that
(\mathcal{GC},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}},\kappa)
is a related triple and that there exists a birational quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{Q}:(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})\dashrightarrow(V,\mathcal{GC})
. Then the following statements hold:

1)

If
\Irr(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0})
and
\Irr(\mathbf{x}_{0}(\kappa))
hold, then
\Irr(\mathbf{x}_{0})
holds;

2)

If in addition to Part
1
,
\Cop(\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{x}^{\prime}_{i})
holds for each cluster variable
\tilde{x}_{i}
in
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
, then
\Cop(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})
holds for each cluster variable
x_{i}\in\mathbf{x}_{0}\setminus\mathbf{x}_{0}(\kappa)
;

3)

If in addition to Parts
1
-
2
,
\Cop(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})
holds for each
x_{i}\in\mathbf{x}_{0}(\kappa)
, then
\bar{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{GC})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(V)
;

4)

If in addition to Parts
1
-
3
, the matrices
B
and
\tilde{B}
are of full rank, as well as
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})\supseteq\mathcal{O}(\widetilde{V})
and for each
i\in\mathcal{I}(\kappa)
,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\prime})\supseteq\mathcal{O}(V)
, then
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{GC})=\mathcal{O}(V)
.

The next result corresponds to the case when a pair of complementary birational quasi-isomorphisms is available. Consider another generalized cluster structure
(\widehat{V},\widehat{\mathcal{GC}})
on an affine complex variety
\widehat{V}
.

Definition 2.29
.

Assume that
(\mathcal{GC},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}},\kappa)
and
(\mathcal{GC},\widehat{\mathcal{GC}},\kappa^{\prime})
are related triples and that there exist birational quasi-isomorphisms
\mathcal{Q}:(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})\dashrightarrow(V,{\mathcal{GC}})
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}:(\widehat{V},\widehat{\mathcal{GC}})\dashrightarrow(V,{\mathcal{GC}})
such that
\mathcal{I}(\kappa)\cap\mathcal{I}(\kappa^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Then
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
are called
complementary
.

Proposition 2.30
.

Let
V
,
\widetilde{V}
and
\widehat{V}
be complex affine varieties such that the coordinate rings
\mathcal{O}(V)
,
\mathcal{O}(\widetilde{V})
and
\mathcal{O}(\widehat{V})
are factorial
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, and such that
\mathcal{O}(V)^{*}=\mathcal{O}(\widetilde{V})^{*}=\mathcal{O}(\widehat{V})^{*}=\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Let
(V,\mathcal{GC})
,
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})
and
(\widehat{V},\widehat{\mathcal{GC}})
be generalized cluster structures on the varieties with fixed initial extended seeds
(\mathbf{x}_{0},B,\mathcal{P})
,
(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\tilde{B},\tilde{\mathcal{P}})
and
(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\hat{B},\hat{\mathcal{P}})
. Assume that
(\mathcal{GC},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}},\kappa)
and
(\mathcal{GC},\widehat{\mathcal{GC}},\kappa^{\prime})
are related triples and that there exists a pair of complementary birational quasi-isomorphisms
\mathcal{Q}:(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})\dashrightarrow(V,\mathcal{GC})
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}:(\widehat{V},\widehat{\mathcal{GC}})\dashrightarrow(V,\mathcal{GC})
. Then the following statements hold:

1)

If
\Irr(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0})
and
\Irr(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0})
hold, then
\Irr(\mathbf{x}_{0})
holds as well;

2)

If in addition to Part
1
, for each cluster variable
\tilde{x}_{i}
in
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0}(\kappa)
and
\hat{x}_{j}
in
\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0}(\kappa^{\prime})
,
\Cop(\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i}^{\prime})
and
\Cop(\hat{x}_{j},\hat{x}_{j}^{\prime})
hold, then for each cluster variable
x_{k}\in\mathbf{x}_{0}
,
\Cop(x_{k},x_{k}^{\prime})
; moreover,
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{GC})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(V)
;

3)

In addition to Parts
1
-
2
, assume that the matrices
B
,
\tilde{B}
and
\hat{B}
are of full rank, as well as
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{GC}})\supseteq\mathcal{O}(\widetilde{V})
and
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widehat{\mathcal{GC}})\supseteq\mathcal{O}(\widehat{V})
. Then
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{GC})=\mathcal{O}(V)
.

3
Poisson properties of the map
\mathcal{Q}

Let
G
be a connected complex reductive Lie group and
\mathfrak{g}
be its Lie algebra with a fixed set of simple roots
\Pi
and a nondegenerate invariant symmetric bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. We decompose
\mathfrak{g}
as
\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{n}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{+}
, where
\mathfrak{n}_{\pm}
are nilpotent subalgebras and
\mathfrak{h}
is a Cartan subalgebra. We set
\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}:=\mathfrak{n}_{\pm}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\pm}
,
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\pm}
to be the connected subgroups of
G
that correspond to
\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}
,
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{\pm}
. For a generic element
U\in G
, we set
U_{\oplus}\in\mathcal{B}_{+}
and
U_{-}\in\mathcal{N}_{-}
to be the elements such that
U=U_{\oplus}U_{-}
. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}:=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
be a Belavin-Drinfeld triple associated with
\Pi
. Since
\gamma^{*}:\mathfrak{n}_{-}\rightarrow\mathfrak{n}_{-}
is a Lie algebra homomorphism, it integrates to a group homomorphism
\tilde{\gamma}^{*}:\mathcal{N}_{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{-}
. We set
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
to be the Poisson bivector (
2.17
) associated with
\mathbf{\Gamma}

and
\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger}
to be the Poisson bivector associated with the trivial BD triple. We define the rational map
\mathcal{Q}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
via

\mathcal{Q}(U):=\rho(U)^{-1}U\rho(U),\ \ \rho(U):=\prod_{i=1}^{\rightarrow}\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-}),\ U\in G.

(3.1)

Note that since
\gamma^{*}
is nilpotent, the above product is finite. The objective of this section is to prove that the map
\mathcal{Q}
is a Poisson isomorphism provided the choices of
r_{0}
in
\pi^{\dagger}_{\text{std}}
and
\pi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
coincide. In Section
4
, we will show that
\mathcal{Q}
is a birational quasi-isomorphism when
G\in\{\SL_{n},\GL_{n}\}
.
12
12

12

We expect that for a connected reductive complex group
G
, there exists
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},G)
compatible with
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
and such that
\mathcal{Q}
is a birational quasi-isomorphism as well.
In Section
5
, we will apply the obtained result in order to conclude that
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
is compatible with
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
.

The choice of trivialization.

In this section, all computations are performed in the right trivialization of
G
. In particular, given a smooth map
f:G\rightarrow H
between Lie groups
G
and
H
, its differential
d^{R}f
in the right trivialization is given by

d_{U}^{R}f(x):=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}f(\exp(tx)U)f(U)^{-1},\ \ U\in G,\ x\in\mathfrak{g},

(3.2)

where
I
is the identity element.
The following properties of
d^{R}
will be used below: if
G,H,K
are Lie groups and
g:G\rightarrow H
and
h:H\rightarrow K
are smooth maps, then the product rule for
d_{U}^{R}
reads
d_{U}^{R}[f\cdot g](x)=d_{U}^{R}f(x)+\Ad_{f(U)}d_{U}^{R}g(x)
, and the chain rule
d_{U}^{R}[h\circ f](x)=d_{f(U)}^{R}h\circ d_{U}^{R}f(x)
,
x\in\mathfrak{g}
. We also set

\Ad_{U}^{\circ}:=\Ad_{U}-\id,\ \ U\in G.

(3.3)

For a given BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, the Poisson bivector
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
in the right trivialization is given by

(\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger,R})_{U}:=[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}^{\circ}](r_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})-[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}](\Omega).

(3.4)

3.1
Preliminary identities

In this subsection, we list various auxiliary identities that are referenced in the proofs below. We let
\pi_{>}
,
\pi_{0}
,
\pi_{<}
,
\pi_{\geq}
,
\pi_{\leq}
to be the orthogonal projections upon
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
,
\mathfrak{h}
,
\mathfrak{n}_{-}
,
\mathfrak{b}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{-}
, respectively, and we let
\Omega_{>}
,
\Omega_{0}
,
\Omega_{<}
,
\Omega_{\geq}
and
\Omega_{\leq}
to be the
2
-tensors that correspond to the projections with respect to the chosen form on
\mathfrak{g}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any
N\in\mathcal{N}_{-}
, the following identities hold:

\displaystyle[(\pi_{0}\circ\Ad_{N})\otimes 1](\Omega_{<})=[1\otimes(\pi_{<}\circ\Ad_{N^{-1}})](\Omega_{0});

(3.5)

\displaystyle[(\pi_{\geq}\circ\Ad_{N})\otimes\Ad_{N}](\Omega_{<})+[1\otimes(\pi_{<}\circ\Ad_{N})](\Omega_{0})=\Omega_{<};

(3.6)

\displaystyle[\Ad_{N}\otimes\Ad_{N}](\Omega_{>})+[(\pi_{<}\circ\Ad_{N})\otimes\Ad_{N}](\Omega_{\leq})=\Omega_{>}.

(3.7)

Proof.

Formula (
3.5
) is straightforward. To verify (
3.6
), we first use (
3.5
) to derive

\begin{split}[(\pi_{0}\circ\Ad_{N})\otimes\Ad_{N}](\Omega_{<})=[1\otimes\Ad_{N}]\circ[1\otimes(\pi_{<}\circ\Ad_{N^{-1}})](\Omega_{0})=\Omega_{0}-[1\otimes\Ad_{N}](\Omega_{0});\end{split}

now, using the latter relation together with
[(\pi_{>}\circ\Ad_{N})\otimes\Ad_{N}](\Omega_{<})=\Omega_{<}
, we see that

\begin{split}[(\pi_{\geq}\circ\Ad_{N})\otimes\Ad_{N}](\Omega_{<})+[1\otimes(\pi_{<}&\circ\Ad_{N})](\Omega_{0})=\\
=&\Omega_{<}+\Omega_{0}-[1\otimes\Ad_{N}](\Omega_{0})+[1\otimes(\pi_{<}\circ\Ad_{N})](\Omega_{0}).\end{split}

The last three terms cancel each other out, and hence (
3.6
) holds. Formula (
3.7
) follows after rewriting
\Omega_{\leq}
as
\Omega_{\leq}=\Omega-\Omega_{>}
and noticing that
\Omega_{>}\in\mathfrak{n}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{n}_{+}
and
[(\pi_{<}\circ\Ad_{N})\otimes\Ad_{N}](\Omega)=\Omega_{>}
.
∎

Let us define rational maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}:G\dashrightarrow G,

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \mathcal{B}(U)=U_{\oplus};

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}:G\dashrightarrow G,

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \mathcal{N}(U)=U_{-}.

Lemma 3.2
.

The following identities hold:

\displaystyle d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}\Ad_{U}=\Ad_{U_{-}}\pi_{<}+\pi_{<}\Ad_{U_{-}}\pi_{>}+\Ad^{\circ}_{U_{-}}\pi_{0};

(3.8)

\displaystyle d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}\pi_{>}=0.

(3.9)

Proof.

Differentiating
\mathcal{N}(U)=[\mathcal{B}(U)]^{-1}\cdot U
, we see that
d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}(\cdot)=\pi_{<}[\Ad_{\,\hskip-3.0pt\left.U_{\oplus}\hskip-4.0pt\right.^{-1}}(\cdot)]
. The identities follow from the latter formula.
∎

3.2
First computations

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be any Belavin-Drinfeld triple of type
A_{n-1}
. In this subsection, we compute some of the first formulas for the proof that
\mathcal{Q}
is a Poisson isomorphism. We will assume that
r_{0}
is the same for both
\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger}
and
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
.

The Poisson bivectors.

We will work with the Poisson bivectors
\pi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
and
\pi^{\dagger}_{\text{std}}
in the right trivialization of
G
in the following form:

\begin{split}(\pi^{\dagger,R}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})_{U}=&[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}](r_{0})-[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}](\Omega_{0})+\\
+&[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}]\left[\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right](\Omega_{>})-[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes 1]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right](\Omega_{>})-\\
-&[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}]\left[1\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right](\Omega_{<})+[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes 1]\left[1\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right](\Omega_{<});\end{split}

(3.10)

\begin{split}(\pi^{\dagger,R}_{\text{std}})_{U}=[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}^{\circ}](r_{0})-[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}&\otimes\Ad_{U}](\Omega_{0})-\\
&-[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}](\Omega_{<})-[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes 1](\Omega_{>}).\end{split}

(3.11)

The objective is to prove that
\mathcal{Q}_{*}\left((\pi^{\dagger,R}_{\text{std}})_{U}\right)=(\pi^{\dagger,R}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

The following identities hold:

\displaystyle\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\Ad_{U}

\displaystyle=\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}};

(3.12)

\displaystyle\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\Ad_{U}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}.

Proof.

The identities follow from the definition of
\mathcal{Q}
(see formula (
3.1
)).
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

The differential of the map
\rho(U)=\prod_{i=1}^{\rightarrow}(\tilde{\gamma}^{*})^{i}(U_{-})
is given by

\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}d_{U}^{R}\rho(\cdot)=\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}(d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}(\cdot)).

(3.13)

Proof.

Recall that
\rho(U)
satisfies the relation

\rho(U)=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(\rho(U)).

(3.14)

Differentiating (
3.14
) and applying
\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}
to both sides, we see that

\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}d_{U}^{R}\rho=\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}+\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})}\gamma^{*}d_{U}^{R}\rho.

(3.15)

The last term can be rewritten as

\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})}\gamma^{*}d_{U}^{R}\rho=\Ad_{\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(\rho(U))}\gamma^{*}d_{U}^{R}\rho=\gamma^{*}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}d_{U}^{R}\rho.

(3.16)

Plugging (
3.16
) back into (
3.15
) and solving for
d_{U}^{R}\rho
, the result follows.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

The differential of
\mathcal{Q}
is given by

d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}=\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}+\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\left[\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}(\cdot)\right].

(3.17)

Proof.

Differentiating the product
\mathcal{Q}(U)=\rho(U)^{-1}U\rho(U)
, we see that

d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}=-\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}d_{U}^{R}\rho+\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}+\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\Ad_{U}d_{U}^{R}\rho.

Rewriting the third term as
\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\Ad_{U}d_{U}^{R}\rho=\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}d_{U}^{R}\rho
and applying Lemma
3.4
to the first and the third terms, we obtain (
3.17
).
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

The following identities hold:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}(\Ad_{U}\pi_{<}(\cdot))

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\pi_{<}(\cdot);

(3.18)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}(\Ad_{U}\pi_{0}(\cdot))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\pi_{<}[\gamma^{*},\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}]\pi_{0}(\cdot)

(3.19)

where
[\gamma^{*},\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}]=\gamma^{*}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}-\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}
.

Proof.

The first identity follows from formula (
3.8
) and formula (
3.14
). As for the second formula, an application of (
3.8
) yields

\begin{split}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}\Ad_{U}\pi_{0}=&\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\Ad_{U_{-}}^{\circ}\pi_{0}=\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\left(\Ad_{\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})}\gamma^{*}-\gamma^{*}\right)\pi_{0}=\\
=&\left(\Ad_{\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(\rho(U)^{-1})}\gamma^{*}-\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\right)\pi_{0}=[\gamma^{*},\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}]\pi_{0}.\qed\end{split}

Lemma 3.7
.

The following formula holds:

\begin{split}-\left[d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\otimes d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\right]&[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}](\Omega_{<})=-D_{0}-D_{1}-\\
=-&\left[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}\right]\left[\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega_{<})-\\
-&\left[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\right]\left[\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega_{<})\end{split}

(3.20)

where

\displaystyle D_{0}:=\left[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}\right]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{<}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega_{<});

(3.21)

\displaystyle D_{1}:=\left[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\right]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{<}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<}).

(3.22)

Proof.

Recall that
\Omega_{<}\in\mathfrak{n}_{+}\otimes\mathfrak{n}_{-}
. One applies formula (
3.17
) (the differential of
\mathcal{Q}
) to
\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}
, and then one invokes formulas (
3.8
) and (
3.9
) (the differential of
\mathcal{N}
) with (
3.18
) and (
3.12
).
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

The following formula holds:

\begin{split}-[d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\otimes d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}]&[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes 1](\Omega_{>})=D_{0}-[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega_{>})+\\
&+[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}]\left[\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega_{>})+\\
&+[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\pi_{<}[\gamma^{*},\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}]\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega_{0}),\end{split}

(3.23)

where
D_{0}
is defined in (
3.21
).

Proof.

Applying formula (
3.17
) to
-[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes 1]
and using formula (
3.9
), we see that

\begin{split}-[d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\otimes d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}][\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes 1](\Omega_{>})=&-[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega_{>})+\\
&+[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega).\end{split}

(3.24)

Since
\Omega=[\Ad_{U}\otimes\Ad_{U}](\Omega)
, the last term can be expanded as

\begin{split}[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1]&\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega)=D_{0}+\\
+&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega_{>})+\\
+&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\Ad^{\circ}_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\right](\Omega_{0}).\end{split}

(3.25)

Now one can apply (
3.18
) and (
3.19
) to (
3.25
) and combine the result with formula (
3.24
).
∎

3.3
Pushforward of the diagonal part of
\pi^{\dagger}

The objective of this subsection is to compute the term

[d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\otimes d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}]\left([\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}^{\circ}](r_{0})-[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}](\Omega_{0})\right).

(3.26)

Let us define the orthogonal projections
\pi_{\Gamma_{1}}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma_{1}}
and
\pi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\perp}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

The following identities hold:

\displaystyle[1\otimes(1-\gamma^{*})](r_{0})=[1\otimes\pi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}](r_{0})-[1\otimes\gamma^{*}](\Omega_{0});

(3.27)

\displaystyle[(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes 1](r_{0})=[\pi_{\Gamma_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Gamma_{1}}](\Omega_{0})+[\pi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}\otimes 1](r_{0});

(3.28)

\displaystyle[(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes(1-\gamma^{*})](r_{0})=[\pi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}](r_{0})+[\pi_{\Gamma_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Gamma_{1}}](\Omega_{0})-[1\otimes\gamma^{*}](\Omega_{0}).

(3.29)

Proof.

The formulas follow from (
2.4
).
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

The following identities hold:

\displaystyle d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\pi_{0}=\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\pi_{0}+\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}(1-\gamma^{*})\pi_{0};

(3.30)

\displaystyle d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\Ad_{U}\pi_{0}=\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\pi_{0}+d_{U}\mathcal{Q}\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\pi_{0}.

(3.31)

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
3.2
that
d^{R}_{U}\mathcal{N}\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\pi_{0}=d^{R}_{U}\mathcal{N}\Ad_{U}\pi_{0}
; combining this with formulas (
3.19
),
(
3.17
) and (
3.12
), we see that

\begin{split}d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\pi_{0}=\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}&\pi_{0}+\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\pi_{0}-\\
-&\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{0}.\end{split}

(3.32)

Now, formula (
3.30
) follows after noticing that

\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\pi_{0}=\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\pi_{0}+\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\pi_{0}

(3.33)

and combining the terms. As for formula (
3.31
), it follows from the fact that
d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{N}\pi_{0}=0
and formula (
3.17
).
∎

Lemma 3.11
.

The following identity holds:

\begin{split}[d_{U}^{R}&\mathcal{Q}\otimes d^{R}_{U}\mathcal{Q}][\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}^{\circ}](r_{0})=[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}](r_{0})-\\
-&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[1\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][1\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][1\otimes\gamma^{*}](\Omega_{0})+\\
+&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes 1][\pi_{\Gamma_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Gamma_{1}}](\Omega_{0})-\\
-&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes\gamma^{*}](\Omega_{0}).\end{split}

(3.34)

Proof.

It follows from formula (
3.30
) that

\begin{split}[d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}&\otimes d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}][\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}^{\circ}](r_{0})=[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}](r_{0})+\\
&+[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[1\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][1\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][1\otimes(1-\gamma^{*})](r_{0})+\\
&+[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes 1][(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes 1](r_{0})+\\
&+[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}]\circ\\
&\hskip 216.81pt\circ[(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes(1-\gamma^{*})](r_{0}).\end{split}

(3.35)

Now formula (
3.34
) follows from applying the identities (
3.27
), (
3.28
) and (
3.29
) to formula (
3.35
) and combining the terms (in the process, note also that
\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\pi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}\pi_{0}=0
).
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

The following identity holds:

\begin{split}[d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\otimes d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}]&\left([\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}^{\circ}](r_{0})-[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}](\Omega_{0})\right)=\\
=&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}](r_{0})-[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}](\Omega_{0})-D_{2}-\\
-&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}]\left[1\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][1\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{0})+\\
+&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1]\left[1\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][1\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{0})+\\
+&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes 1][\gamma^{*}\otimes 1](\Omega_{0})-\\
-&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes 1](\Omega_{0})\end{split}

(3.36)

where

D_{2}:=[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes 1](\Omega_{0}).

(3.37)

Proof.

Notice that

\begin{split}[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}](\Omega_{0}&)+[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1][1\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{0})=\\
=&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}](\Omega_{0})+[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1][1\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{0}).\end{split}

(3.38)

Let us expand
[d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\otimes d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}][\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}](\Omega_{0})
via (
3.30
) and (
3.31
), and apply (
3.38
) to the resulting expression. We obtain

\begin{split}-[d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}\otimes d_{U}^{R}\mathcal{Q}]&[\Ad_{U}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{U}](\Omega_{0})=-[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}](\Omega_{0})-\\
-&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1][1\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{0})-\\
-&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[1\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][1\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][1\otimes(1-\gamma^{*})](\Omega_{0})-\\
-&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes 1][(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes 1](\Omega_{0})-\\
-&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes 1](\Omega_{0})-\\
-&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}]\circ\\
&\hskip 173.44756pt\circ[(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes(1-\gamma^{*})](\Omega_{0}).\end{split}

(3.39)

Now, let us indicate how the terms in (
3.34
) and in (
3.39
) combine into the terms of (
3.36
). The term
-D_{2}
in (

) is obtained via combining the last term in (

) with the last term in (

). The fourth and the fifth terms in (

) with the second and the third terms in (

). To obtain the sixth term in (

), together with the fact that
\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\pi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}\pi_{0}=0
. The last term in (

).
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

The following identity holds:

\begin{split}-D_{1}-D_{2}=&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})+\\
+&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[1\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})+\\
+&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})+\\
+&[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][\gamma^{*}\otimes 1](\Omega_{0}).\end{split}

(3.40)

where
D_{1}
is given by (
3.22
) and

is given by (

).

Proof.

For convenience, let us drop the operator
[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]
from the computations. A part of

can be rewritten in the following way:

\begin{split}\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right.\left.\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right]&[\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{<}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})=\\
=&\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})-\\
-&\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{0}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})-\\
-&\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{>}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<}).\end{split}

(3.41)

). To rewrite the first term, we apply formula (

) and (

):

\begin{split}\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right]&[\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})=\\

(3.42)

(3.43)

\begin{split}-\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right]&[\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{0}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})=\\
=&-\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\pi_{<}\Ad_{\,\hskip-3.0pt\left.U_{-}\hskip-4.0pt\right.^{-1}}](\Omega_{0});\end{split}

(3.44)

then, we write

(3.45)

together with formula (

\begin{split}-\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right]&[\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{>}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})=\\
=&-\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}\,\hskip-3.0pt\left.U_{-}\hskip-4.0pt\right.^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})=\\

(3.46)

\begin{split}\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right.\left.\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right]&[\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{<}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})=\\

+&\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][\gamma^{*}\otimes 1](\Omega_{0})-\\
-&\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][\gamma^{*}\otimes\gamma^{*}](\Omega_{\leq}).\end{split}

(3.47)

Now, let us turn to

\begin{split}-\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right]&[\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes 1](\Omega_{0})=\\

&+\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][\gamma^{*}\otimes 1](\Omega_{0}).\end{split}

(3.48)

The negative of (
3.47
) together with (
3.48
) combine into

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right.\left.\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{<}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})-

(3.49)

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][(1-\gamma^{*})\otimes 1](\Omega_{0})=

\displaystyle=-\left[\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})+

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][\gamma^{*}\otimes\gamma^{*}](\Omega_{<})+

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}][\gamma^{*}\otimes 1](\Omega_{0}).

Let us write

\begin{split}\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right]&[\pi_{\Gamma_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Gamma_{1}}]-\left[\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right]=\\
=&\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}]+1\otimes 1+\left[\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right]+\left[1\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right],\end{split}

(3.50)

and let us observe that
\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\pi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}\pi_{>}=0
, so (

) can be updated as

\begin{split}&-\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right.\left.\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\gamma^{*}\pi_{<}\Ad_{U_{-}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})-\\

&\hskip 79.49744pt=[\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})+\\
&\hskip 82.38774pt\hphantom{=}+\left[1\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})+\\
&\hskip 82.38774pt\hphantom{=}+\left[\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right][\pi_{<}\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}\otimes\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}](\Omega_{<})+\\

(3.51)

Applying
[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}]
, we finally obtain (

).
∎

3.4
Proof of Proposition
1.4

Let us restate Proposition
1.4
for convenience:

Proposition
.

If the
r_{0}
parts of
\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger}
and
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
coincide, then the rational map
\mathcal{Q}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
is Poisson.

Proof.

We apply the pushforward
\mathcal{Q}_{*}
to each term of (
3.11
) and combine the output in the form (
3.10
). The strategy is to collect the terms (
3.20
), (
3.23
), (
3.36
) and (
3.40
), and then apply either (
3.6
) or (
3.7
) to cancel out
\Ad_{\rho(U)^{-1}}
from the resulting expressions. Let us notice first that all the terms that end with
[\gamma^{*}\otimes 1](\Omega_{0})
cancel each other out (this includes a part of the last term in (
3.23
)). As for the rest, let us indicate the correspondence between the terms in the formulas (
3.20
), (
3.23
), (
3.36
) and (
3.40
), and the terms in (
3.10
).

1)

To obtain the term
[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}]\left[\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right](\Omega_{>})
, one combines the last two terms of (
3.23
) with the
[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}]
part of the penultimate term in (
3.40
), and then one applies (
3.7
);

2)

To obtain the term
-[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1]\left[\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\otimes 1\right](\Omega_{>})
, one combines the first term from (
3.23
), the last term from (
3.36
) and the
[\Ad^{\circ}_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}\otimes 1]
parts from the first and the penultimate terms of (
3.40
); then, one applies (
3.7
);

3)

To obtain the term
-[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}]\left[1\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right](\Omega_{<})
, one combines the
[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}]
parts of the first two terms in (
3.20
), the first two terms in (
3.40
) and the fourth term in (
3.36
); then, one applies (
3.6
);

4)

To obtain the term
[\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1]\left[1\otimes\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right](\Omega_{<})
, one combines the fifth term from (
3.36
) together with the
\Ad_{\mathcal{Q}(U)}^{\circ}\otimes 1
parts of the second term of (
3.20
) and the second term of (
3.40
); then, one applies (
3.6
).

Thus the proposition is proved.
∎

3.5
An alternative to
\mathcal{Q}

In this subsection, we define an alternative version of
\mathcal{Q}
for the
g
-convention
(see Section
7
). Continuing in the setup of the current section, let us decompose a generic element
U\in G
into the product
U=U_{+}U_{\ominus}
where
U_{+}\in\mathcal{N}_{+}
and
U_{\ominus}\in\mathcal{B}_{-}
. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}:=(\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{1},\gamma^{*})
be the opposite BD triple. Define the rational map
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
via

\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}(U):=\rho^{\mathrm{op}}(U)U\rho^{\mathrm{op}}(U)^{-1},\ \ \rho^{\mathrm{op}}(U):=\prod_{i=1}^{\leftarrow}\tilde{\gamma}(U_{+}),\ U\in G.

(3.52)

Define a map
T_{*}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
via setting
T_{*}|_{\mathfrak{h}}=\id|_{\mathfrak{h}}
,
T_{*}(e_{-\alpha})=e_{\alpha}
,
T_{*}(e_{\alpha})=e_{-\alpha}
, where
\langle e_{\alpha},e_{-\alpha}\rangle=1
, as in Section
2.3
. Then
T_{*}
integrates to the
transposition map

T:G\rightarrow G
.

Lemma 3.14
.

Pick any
r_{0}
element for
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
and set
r_{0}^{t}
to be the
r_{0}
component for
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}^{\dagger}
. Then the transposition map
T:(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})\rightarrow(G,-\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}^{\dagger})
is a Poisson isomorphism.

Proof.

It follows from formula (
2.5
) that the classical
r
-matrix
r_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
can be expressed as

r_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}=r_{0}+\left[1\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\right](\Omega_{>})-\left[\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\otimes 1\right](\Omega_{<}).

(3.53)

We see that

[T_{*}\otimes T_{*}](r_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})=r_{0}+\left[1\otimes\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\right](\Omega_{<})-\left[\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\otimes 1\right](\Omega_{>})=(r_{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}})^{t}.

(3.54)

Also,
d_{U}^{R}T(\Ad_{U}x)=T_{*}(x)
and
d_{U}^{R}T(\Ad_{U}^{\circ}x)=-\Ad_{T(U)}^{\circ}T_{*}(x)
,
x\in\mathfrak{g}
. Now, applying
[d_{U}^{R}T\otimes d_{U}^{R}T]
to formula (
3.4
), we conclude that
[d_{U}^{R}T\otimes d_{U}^{R}T]((\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger,R})_{U})=-(\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}^{\dagger,R})_{T(U)}
.
∎

Proposition 3.15
.

If the
r_{0}
parts of
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
and
\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger}
are the same, then the rational map
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
is Poisson.

Proof.

Indeed, let
\mathcal{Q}:(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\rightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}^{\dagger})
be defined relative the opposite BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Choose an arbitrary
r_{0}
for
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
and set
r_{0}^{t}
to be the similar component for
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}^{\dagger}
. Now notice that the diagram

\textstyle{(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{(G,-\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Q}}
\textstyle{(G,-\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}^{\dagger})}

(3.55)

is commutative. Thus
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a Poisson isomorphism.
∎

4
Birational quasi-isomorphisms

In this section, we describe various birational quasi-isomorphisms between the generalized cluster structures in the
h
-convention in type
A_{n-1}
(see the description in Section
1.2
). More specifically, for a BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}:=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
of type
A_{n-1}
and
G\in\{\SL_{n},\GL_{n}\}
, we describe the following maps:

•

The birational map
\mathcal{F}:(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})
, which is a basic ingredient for defining the
h
-variables and birational quasi-isomorphisms;

•

The birational quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{Q}:(G,\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}))\dashrightarrow(G,\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}))
and its inverse
\mathcal{F}^{c}:=\mathcal{Q}^{-1}
. The results of Section
3
show that
\mathcal{Q}:(\GL_{n},\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
is a Poisson isomorphism if the
r_{0}
parts of
\pi^{\dagger}_{\text{std}}
and
\pi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
are the same;

•

For any
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}
, we construct a birational quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{G}:(G,\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}))\dashrightarrow(G,\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}))
; as a map
\mathcal{G}:(G,\pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
, it is a Poisson isomorphism if the
r_{0}
parts of
\pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}
and
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
are the same. If
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is obtained from
\mathbf{\Gamma}
via removal of a pair of roots, we provide an even more explicit description of
\mathcal{G}
in Section
4.5
.

If
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}
are BD triples of type
A_{n-1}
, we will decorate the
h
-variables in
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}_{h}(\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
with a tilde, to distinguish them from the
h
-variables in
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
. The marker (see Definition
2.20
) will always be chosen in such a way that each
\varphi_{ij}
and
c_{i}
are related to themselves, and each
h_{ij}
is related to
\tilde{h}_{ij}
. Then the marked variables in
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
are given by

\{h_{i+1,i+1}\ |\ i\in\Gamma_{2}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}_{2}\}.

4.1
Belavin-Drinfeld data for
\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})

In this section, we introduce various notions and formulas pertaining to Belavin-Drinfeld triples.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
be a Belavin-Drinfeld triple. The
degree of nilpotency
of
\gamma
is defined as the number

\deg\gamma:=\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma_{1}}\{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\ |\ \gamma^{m}(\alpha)\notin\Gamma_{1}\}.

(4.1)

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}:=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
and
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}:=(\tilde{\Gamma}_{1},\tilde{\Gamma}_{2},\theta)
be two Belavin-Drinfeld triples. We write
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}
if
\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}\subset\Gamma_{1}
,
\tilde{\Gamma}_{2}\subset\Gamma_{2}
and
\gamma|_{\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}}=\theta
, and we say that
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is obtained from
\mathbf{\Gamma}
via removal of the roots

\Gamma_{1}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}
from
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}_{2}
from
\Gamma_{2}
.

Runs and orientability of
\gamma
.

Let us fix a set of simple roots
\Pi
of type
A_{n-1}
and identify it with the interval
[1,n-1]
. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}:=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
be a BD triple of type
A_{n-1}
. For an arbitrary
i\in\Pi
, set

i_{-}:=\max\{j\in[0,n]\setminus\Gamma_{1}\ |\ j<i\},\ \ i_{+}:=\min\{j\in[1,n]\setminus\Gamma_{1}\ |\ j\geq i\}.

(4.2)

An
X
-run
of
i
is the interval
\Delta(i):=[i_{-}+1,i_{+}]
. Replacing
\Gamma_{1}
with
\Gamma_{2}
in the above formulas, we obtain the definition of a
Y
-run

\bar{\Delta}(i)
of
i
. The
X
-runs partition the set
[1,n]
, and likewise the
Y
-runs. A run is called
trivial
if it consists of a single element. Evidently, the map
\gamma
can be viewed as a bijection between the set of nontrivial
X
-runs and the set of nontrivial
Y
-runs. We say that a
Y
-run
\bar{\Delta}

corresponds
to an
X
-run
\Delta
if
\gamma(i)=j
for some
i\in\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}
and some
j\in\bar{\Delta}\cap\Gamma_{2}
. The notion of runs was introduced in
[
23
]
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
be a BD triple of type
A_{n-1}
, and let
\Delta
be a nontrivial
X
-run. We say that
\gamma
is
positively oriented on
\Delta
if
\gamma(i+1)=\gamma(i)+1
for each
i,i+1\in\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}
.
Likewise, if
\gamma(i+1)=\gamma(i)-1
for each
i,i+1\in\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}
, then
\gamma
is called
negatively oriented on
\Delta
. The former circumstance will be denoted as
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}>0
, while the latter as
13
13

13

If an
X
-run
\Delta
consists of
2
elements, we will view
\gamma
as being positively oriented on
\Delta
. However, the distinction is not necessary in this case.

\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0
.

Remark 4.4
.

In
[
15
,
23
]
, a BD triple was called
oriented
if
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}>0
for every nontrivial
X
-run
\Delta
; it was called
nonoriented
if there exists a nontrivial
X
-run
\Delta
such that
\gamma_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0
.

The map of indices
\bar{\gamma}
.

Let us set
\bar{\Gamma}_{1}
to be the union of all the nontrivial
X
-runs
14
14

14

Alternatively,
\bar{\Gamma}_{1}
is the union of
\Gamma_{1}
and all the right endpoints of all the nontrivial
X
-runs. Similarly for
\bar{\Gamma}_{2}
.
and
\bar{\Gamma}_{2}
to be the union of all the nontrivial
Y
-runs. We define a map
\bar{\gamma}:\bar{\Gamma}_{1}\rightarrow\bar{\Gamma}_{2}
in the following way: for each nontrivial
X
-run
\Delta
and the corresponding
Y
-run
\bar{\Delta}
,

a)

If
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}>0
, then the restriction
\bar{\gamma}|_{\Delta}:\Delta\rightarrow\bar{\Delta}
is set to be the unique increasing bijection;

b)

If
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0
, then the restriction
\bar{\gamma}|_{\Delta}:\Delta\rightarrow\bar{\Delta}
is set to be the unique decreasing bijection
15
15

15

Note that if
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}>0
, then
\bar{\gamma}|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}=\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}
; however, if
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0
, then
\gamma(i)+1=\bar{\gamma}(i)
,
i\in\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}
.
.

A similar recipe applies to
\bar{\gamma}^{*}
.

The map of subsets
\check{\gamma}
.

Let
\Delta
be any nontrivial
X
-run and
\bar{\Delta}
be the corresponding
Y
-run. We define the map
\check{\gamma}:2^{\Delta}\rightarrow 2^{\Delta}
in the following way. For any
A=\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}\subseteq\Delta
,

a)

If
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}>0
, then
\check{\gamma}(A):=\{\bar{\gamma}(a_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k}
;

b)

If
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0
, then
\check{\gamma}(A):=\bar{\Delta}\setminus\{\bar{\gamma}(a_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k}
.

The extension
\mathring{\gamma}:\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

As was shown in
[
23
]
, in the case of
\GL_{n}
, it is expedient to extend the BD map
\gamma:\Gamma_{1}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2}
in a different way
16
16

16

In our previous work
[
31
]
, we denoted the resulting map by
\gamma
as well, for the main focus of the paper was the case of
\GL_{n}
. In this paper, we keep the original notation introduced in
[
23
]
.
. Let
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k}
be the list of all nontrivial
X
-runs, and set
\bar{\Delta}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\Delta}_{k}
to be the list of the corresponding
Y
-runs (in other words,
\gamma(\Delta_{i}\cap\Gamma_{1})=\bar{\Delta}_{i}\cap\Gamma_{2}
,
i\in[1,k]
). Set
\gl(\Delta_{i})
to be a subalgebra of
\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
of the matrices that are zero outside of the block
\Delta_{i}\times\Delta_{i}
(and similarly for
\gl(\bar{\Delta}_{i})
). For each
i\in[1,k]
define a map
\mathring{\gamma}_{i}:\gl(\Delta_{i})\rightarrow\gl(\bar{\Delta}_{i})
in the following way: first, canonically identify
\gl(\bar{\Delta}_{i})
and
\gl(\Delta_{i})
with
\gl_{|\Delta_{i}|}(\mathbb{C})
; second,

a)

If
\gamma|_{\Delta_{i}\cap\Gamma_{1}}>0
, then
\mathring{\gamma}_{i}:\gl(\Delta_{i})\rightarrow\gl(\bar{\Delta}_{i})
acts as the identity map under the identifications
\gl(\Delta_{i})\cong\gl_{|\Delta_{i}|}(\mathbb{C})
and
\gl(\bar{\Delta}_{i})\cong\gl_{|\Delta_{i}|}(\mathbb{C})
; in other words,
\mathring{\gamma}_{i}(e_{ks})=e_{\bar{\gamma}(k)\bar{\gamma}(s)}
,
k,s\in\Delta_{i}
;

b)

If
\gamma|_{\Delta_{i}\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0
, then let us set
\omega_{0}:=\sum_{j=1}^{|\Delta_{i}|}(-1)^{j-1}e_{|\Delta_{i}|-j+1,j}
; under the identifications, if
A\in\gl_{|\Delta_{i}|}(\mathbb{C})
, then
\mathring{\gamma}_{i}:\gl(\Delta_{i})\rightarrow\gl(\bar{\Delta}_{i})
acts as

\mathring{\gamma}_{i}(A)=\omega_{0}(-A)^{T}\omega_{0}^{-1}

(4.3)

where
A^{T}
is the transpose of
A
. In other words,
\mathring{\gamma}_{i}(e_{ks})=(-1)^{s+k-1}e_{\bar{\gamma}(s)\bar{\gamma}(k)}
,
k,s\in\Delta_{i}
.

The map
\mathring{\gamma}:\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is defined as the direct sum
\mathring{\gamma}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\mathring{\gamma}_{i}
extended by zero to
\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. Similarly, one defines the pieces
\mathring{\gamma}_{i}^{*}:\gl_{n}(\bar{\Delta}_{i})\rightarrow\gl_{n}(\Delta_{i})
, and then the map
\mathring{\gamma}^{*}:\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is obtained as the direct sum
\mathring{\gamma}^{*}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\mathring{\gamma}_{i}^{*}
extended by zero to
\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

The group homomorphism
{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}
.

In the case of
\GL_{n}
, the restrictions of
\mathring{\gamma}
to the Borel subalgebras
\mathfrak{b}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{-}
of
\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
are Lie algebra homomorphisms. Therefore, one can integrate
\mathring{\gamma}
in each case to a group homomorphism
{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}:\mathcal{B}_{\pm}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\pm}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{+}
and
\mathcal{B}_{-}
are the subgroups of upper and lower triangular matrices. Likewise, one integrates
\mathring{\gamma}^{*}:\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}\rightarrow\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}
to a group homomorphism
{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}:\mathcal{B}_{\pm}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\pm}
. Note: since
\gamma
and
\mathring{\gamma}
coincide on
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{-}
,
\tilde{\gamma}(N_{\pm})={\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(N_{\pm})
,
N_{\pm}\in\mathcal{N}_{\pm}
. We will prefer the notation
\tilde{\gamma}
over
{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}
if the argument is a unipotent lower or upper triangular matrix. Next, set

\gl_{n}({\Gamma_{1}}):=\bigoplus_{|\Delta|>1}\gl_{n}(\Delta),\ \ \gl_{n}({\Gamma_{2}}):=\bigoplus_{|\bar{\Delta}|>1}\gl_{n}(\bar{\Delta}),

(4.4)

where
\gl_{n}(\Delta)
and
\gl_{n}(\bar{\Delta})
are defined above. Let
\GL_{n}(\Gamma_{i})
be the connected subgroup of
\GL_{n}
that corresponds to
\gl_{n}(\Gamma_{i})
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. The restriction
\mathring{\gamma}|_{\gl_{n}(\Gamma_{1})}:\gl_{n}(\Gamma_{1})\rightarrow\gl_{n}(\Gamma_{2})
can be integrated to the group homomorphism
{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}:\GL_{n}(\Gamma_{1})\rightarrow\GL_{n}(\Gamma_{2})
; likewise,
\mathring{\gamma}^{*}:\gl_{n}(\Gamma_{2})\rightarrow\gl_{n}(\Gamma_{1})
integrates to
{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}:\GL_{n}(\Gamma_{2})\rightarrow\GL_{n}(\Gamma_{1})
.

Remark 4.5
.

Note that
\mathring{\gamma}|_{\sll_{n}(\mathbb{C})}
coincides with the map
\gamma:\sll_{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\sll_{n}(\mathbb{C})
introduced in Section
2.1
only on the nilpotent subalgebras
\mathfrak{n}_{-}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
: the maps are different on the Cartan subalgebra of
\sll_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. Moreover, the version of
\gamma
from Section
2.1
does not integrate to a homomorphism of Borel subgroups.

Projections.

We equip
\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
with the trace form and define the orthogonal projections
\mathring{\pi}_{\Gamma_{i}}:\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\gl_{n}(\Gamma_{i})
and
\mathring{\pi}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{i}}:=\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\gl_{n}(\Gamma_{i})^{\perp}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. Given the parabolic subalgebras
\mathfrak{p}_{\pm}(\Gamma_{i})
of
\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and the corresponding parabolic subgroups
\mathcal{P}_{\pm}(\Gamma_{i})
, we integrate the restrictions
(\mathring{\pi}_{\Gamma_{i}})|_{\mathfrak{p}_{\pm}(\Gamma_{i})}
to the group projections
\mathring{\Pi}_{\Gamma_{i}}:\mathcal{P}_{\pm}(\Gamma_{i})\rightarrow\GL_{n}(\Gamma_{i})
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. Let
\mathcal{H}
be the Cartan subgroup of
\GL_{n}
,
\mathfrak{h}(\Gamma_{i})
the Cartan subalgebra of
\gl_{n}(\Gamma_{i})
,
\mathcal{H}(\Gamma_{i})
and
\mathcal{H}(\hat{\Gamma}_{i})
be the connected subgroups of
\GL_{n}
that correspond to
\mathfrak{h}(\Gamma_{i})
and
\mathfrak{h}(\Gamma_{i})^{\perp}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. We set
\mathring{\Pi}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{i}}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}(\hat{\Gamma}_{i})
to be the group homomorphism that corresponds to
\mathring{\pi}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{i}}|_{\mathfrak{h}}
.

The Gauss decomposition and
\tilde{\gamma}^{*}
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
be a BD triple of type
A_{n-1}
, and let
U=U_{\oplus}U_{-}
be the decomposition of a generic element
U\in\GL_{n}
into an upper triangular matrix
U_{\oplus}
and a unipotent lower triangular matrix
U_{-}
. Let
\Delta
be a nontrivial
X
-run and
i,j\in\Delta
,
i>j
. We will need the following formulas:

a)

If
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}>0
, then

[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})]_{ij}=\frac{\det U^{\{\bar{\gamma}(j)\}\cup[\bar{\gamma}(i)+1,n]}_{[\bar{\gamma}(i),n]}}{\det U^{[\bar{\gamma}(i),n]}_{[\bar{\gamma}(i),n]}};

(4.5)

b)

If
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0
, then

[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})]_{ij}=\frac{\det U^{[\bar{\gamma}(i),n]\setminus\{\bar{\gamma}(j)\}}_{[\bar{\gamma}(i)+1,n]}}{\det U^{[\bar{\gamma}(i)+1,n]}_{[\bar{\gamma}(i)+1,n]}}.

(4.6)

The above formulas follow from Lemma
C.1
and Lemma
C.2
. Furthermore, if
N
is a unipotent lower triangular matrix and
i,j\in\Delta
such that
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0
, then

[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(N)]_{ij}=(-1)^{i+j}[N^{-1}]_{\bar{\gamma}(j)\bar{\gamma}(i)}=\det N^{[1,n]\setminus\{\bar{\gamma}(j)\}}_{[1,n]\setminus\{\bar{\gamma}(i)\}}.

(4.7)

Ringed version of
R_{0}
for
\GL_{n}
.

As in our previous work
[
31
]
, it is expedient to choose
R_{0}
with special properties in the case of
\GL_{n}
. Any such special solution will be denoted as
\mathring{R}_{0}
and called a
ringed

R_{0}
element. The ringed element
\mathring{R}_{0}
is a solution of (
2.8
)-(
2.9
) that in addition satisfies any of the following equivalent identities:

\displaystyle\mathring{R}_{0}(1-\mathring{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\mathring{\pi}_{\Gamma_{1}}+\mathring{R}_{0}\mathring{\pi}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}\ \

\displaystyle\ \ \mathring{R}_{0}(1-\mathring{\gamma}^{*})

\displaystyle=-\mathring{\gamma}^{*}+\mathring{R}_{0}\mathring{\pi}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{2}}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathring{R}_{0}^{*}(1-\mathring{\gamma})

\displaystyle=-\mathring{\gamma}+\mathring{R}_{0}^{*}\mathring{\pi}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}\ \

\displaystyle\ \ \mathring{R}_{0}^{*}(1-\mathring{\gamma}^{*})

\displaystyle=\mathring{\pi}_{\Gamma_{2}}+\mathring{R}_{0}^{*}\mathring{\pi}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{2}}

where all the maps are assumed to be restricted to the diagonal matrices.

4.2
The map
\mathcal{F}

Fix a set of simple roots
\Pi
of type
A_{n-1}
and a BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}:=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
of type
A_{n-1}
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\pm}
be the Borel subgroups and their unipotent radicals, and set
\mathcal{H}
to be the Cartan subgroup. We decompose a generic element
U\in\GL_{n}
as
U=U_{\oplus}U_{-}
, where
U_{-}\in\mathcal{N}_{-}
and
U_{\oplus}\in\mathcal{B}_{+}
. Define a sequence
\mathcal{F}_{k}:\GL_{n}\dashrightarrow\GL_{n}
via

\mathcal{F}_{0}(U):=U,\ \ \mathcal{F}_{k}(U)=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([\mathcal{F}_{k-1}(U)]_{-})U,\ \ U\in\GL_{n},\ k\geq 1.

(4.9)

The rational map
\mathcal{F}:G\dashrightarrow G
is defined as the limit

\mathcal{F}(U):=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{F}_{k}(U),\ \ U\in\GL_{n}.

(4.10)

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
N\in\mathcal{N}_{-}
and
k\in[0,\deg\gamma+1]
. Let
\{\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{i}:\GL_{n}\dashrightarrow\GL_{n}\}_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of rational maps defined via

\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(U):=(\tilde{\gamma}^{*})^{k}(N)U,\ \ \hat{\mathcal{F}}_{i}(U):=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{i-1}(U)]_{-})U,\ \ U\in G,\ i\geq 1.

(4.11)

Then
\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{\deg\gamma+1-k}(U)=\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma+1-k}(U)
.

Proof.

For a subset
A\subseteq\Pi
, set
\mathcal{N}_{-}(A)
to be a subgroup of
\mathcal{N}_{-}
generated by
A
. Define
\mathcal{S}:\mathcal{N}_{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{-}
via setting
\mathcal{S}(N):=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([NU]_{-})
, and let
S_{\ell}:=\{\beta\in\Phi_{+}\ |\ \beta\succ(\gamma^{*})^{\ell}(\alpha)\ \text{for some}\ \alpha\in A\cap\Gamma_{1}\}
, where we imply the usual order on the roots. The statement of the lemma is an observation that the entries of
N
in the matrix
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(N)
are visible only at the root vectors
e_{-\beta}
for
\beta\in S_{\ell}
.
∎

Proposition 4.7
.

The sequence
\mathcal{F}_{k}
stabilizes at
k=\deg\gamma
and
\mathcal{F}(U)=\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma}(U)
. Moreover, the rational map
\mathcal{F}:\GL_{n}\dashrightarrow\GL_{n}
satisfies the relation

\mathcal{F}(U)=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([\mathcal{F}(U)]_{-})U,

(4.12)

and in particular,
\mathcal{F}
is birational, with the inverse given by

\mathcal{F}^{-1}(U)=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})^{-1}U.

(4.13)

Proof.

The proposition follows from Lemma
4.6
via setting
N:=[\mathcal{F}_{i}(U)]_{-}
for
i\geq 1
.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
N\in\mathcal{N}_{-}
and
T\in\mathcal{H}
. Set
N_{0}:=N
and
N_{k}:=(\tilde{\gamma}^{*})^{k}(N)
,
k\in[1,\deg\gamma]
. For any
k\in[0,\deg\gamma]
, the following formulas hold:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(N_{k+1}UN_{k}^{-1})=\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(U)N_{k}^{-1};

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(T)UT^{-1})=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(T)\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(U)T^{-1};

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(T^{-1}U\tilde{\gamma}(T))=T^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(U)\tilde{\gamma}(T).

(4.16)

Proof.

Let us set

\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{i}(U):=\mathcal{F}_{i}(N_{k+1}UN_{k}^{-1})N_{k},\ \ i\geq 0.

Observe that
\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(U)=N_{k+1}U
and that
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}(U)
satisfies the recurrence relation
\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{i}(U)=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{i-1}(U)]_{-})U
,
i\geq 0
. Indeed, from the recurrence relation for
\mathcal{F}_{i}(U)
, we see that

\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{i+1}(U)=\mathcal{F}_{i}(N_{k+1}UN_{k}^{-1})N_{k}=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([\mathcal{F}_{i-1}(N_{k+1}UN_{k}^{-1})]_{-})N_{k+1}UN_{k}^{-1}N_{k}=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{i}(U)]_{-})U.

By Lemma
4.6
, we conclude that
\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{\deg\gamma-k}(U)=\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(U)
, which yields the first formula of the proposition.

The second formula is obvious for
k=\deg\gamma
; the other cases follow easily from induction:

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k+1}({\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)UT^{-1})=&{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}([\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}({\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)UT^{-1})]_{-}){\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)UT^{-1}=\\
=&{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}([{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(U)T^{-1}]_{-}){\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)UT^{-1}=\\
=&{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T[\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(U)]_{-}T^{-1}){\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)UT^{-1}=\\
=&{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k+1}(U)T^{-1}.\end{split}

The last formula is also evident for
k=\deg\gamma
. Assuming it holds for some
k
, we see that

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k+1}(T^{-1}U{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(T))=&{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}\left([\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(T^{-1}U{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(T))]_{-}\right)T^{-1}U{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(T)=\\
=&{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}\left({\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(T^{-1})[\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(U)]_{-}{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(T)\right)T^{-1}U{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(T)=\\
=&\mathring{\Pi}_{\Gamma_{1}}(T^{-1}){\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}\left([\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(U)]_{-}\right)\mathring{\Pi}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}(T^{-1})U{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(T)=\\
=&\mathring{\Pi}_{\Gamma_{1}}(T^{-1})\mathring{\Pi}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}(T^{-1}){\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}\left([\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k}(U)]_{-}\right)U{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(T)=\\
=&T^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{\deg\gamma-k+1}(U){\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(T).\end{split}

Thus the identities hold.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

The following identities hold :

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(N)UN^{-1})=\mathcal{F}(U)N^{-1};

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}({{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}}^{*}(T)UT^{-1}))={{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}}^{*}(T)\mathcal{F}(U)T^{-1};

(4.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(T^{-1}U{{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}}(T))=T^{-1}\mathcal{F}(U){{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}}(T).

(4.19)

Proof.

Set
k=0
in Proposition
4.8
and invoke Proposition
4.7
.∎

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
h_{ij}
be any
h
-variable from the initial extended cluster of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n})
. Then there exist characters
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
such that the following identities hold:

\displaystyle h_{ij}(\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(N)UN^{-1})=h_{ij}(U);

(4.20)

\displaystyle h_{ij}({\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)UT^{-1})=\chi(T)h_{ij}(U);

(4.21)

\displaystyle h_{ij}(T^{-1}U{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}(T))=\chi^{\prime}(T)h_{ij}(U).

(4.22)

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
h_{ij}
be any
h
-variable from the initial extended cluster of
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n})
. Then the following identities hold:

\displaystyle\nabla_{U}h_{ij}\cdot U-\mathring{\gamma}\left(U\cdot\nabla_{U}h_{ij}\right)\in\mathfrak{b}_{-};

(4.23)

\displaystyle\pi_{0}\left(\nabla_{U}\log h_{ij}\cdot U-\mathring{\gamma}\left(U\cdot\nabla_{U}\log h_{ij}\right)\right)=\mathrm{const};

(4.24)

\displaystyle\pi_{0}\left(U\cdot\nabla_{U}\log h_{ij}-\mathring{\gamma}^{*}\left(\nabla_{U}\log h_{ij}\cdot U\right)\right)=\mathrm{const}.

(4.25)

Proof.

The formulas follow from differentiating the identities in Corollary
4.10
.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

The birational map
\mathcal{F}:(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(\GL_{n},\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})
is not a Poisson map. Nevertheless, we believe that the pushforward Poisson bivector
\mathcal{F}_{*}(\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
, as well as the induced compatible generalized cluster structure on
\GL_{n}
might be of interest. We also believe that there might exist generalized cluster structures on some interesting subvarieties of
\GL_{n}
whose variables are constructed as the flag minors of
\mathcal{F}_{m}
(akin to the formula (
1.13
)), and these generalized cluster structures might be compatible with some other Poisson structures. For a discussion of these problems, as well as for supporting evidence the reader might refer to the supplementary file
[
33
]
.

4.3
The maps
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{F}^{c}

Recall from Section
3
that the rational map
\mathcal{Q}:(\GL_{n},\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
is given by

\mathcal{Q}(U):=\rho(U)^{-1}U\rho(U),\ \ \rho(U):=\prod_{i\geq 1}^{\rightarrow}(\tilde{\gamma}^{*})^{i}(U_{-}).

(4.26)

Let us define the rational map
\mathcal{F}^{c}:(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(\GL_{n},\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})
via

\mathcal{F}^{c}(U):=\mathcal{F}(U)U[\mathcal{F}(U)]^{-1}.

(4.27)

Proposition 4.13
.

The rational maps
\mathcal{F}^{c}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are inverse to each other.

Proof.

Recall that
\rho(U)
satisfies the relation

\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(\rho(U))=\rho(U).

Applying formulas (
4.13
) and (
4.17
), we see that

\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Q}(U))=\mathcal{F}\left(\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(\rho(U))^{-1}\left[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})^{-1}U\right]\rho(U)\right)=U\cdot\rho(U);

therefore,

\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{Q}(U))=\left[U\cdot\rho(U)\right]\left[\rho(U)^{-1}U\rho(U)\right]\left[\rho(U)^{-1}U^{-1}\right]=U.\qed

Remark 4.14
.

The map
\mathcal{F}^{c}
can also be written as

\mathcal{F}^{c}(U)=\mathcal{F}(U)\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(U)_{-})^{-1}=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([\mathcal{F}(U)]_{-})U\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([\mathcal{F}(U)]_{-})^{-1}.

(4.28)

The identities follow from (
4.12
).

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
\psi
be any variable from the initial extended cluster of
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
. Then the following identities hold:

1)

If
\psi
is a
\varphi
- or
c
-function, then
\mathcal{Q}^{*}(\psi(U))=\psi(U)
;

2)

If
\psi=h_{ij}
for some
h
-function
h_{ij}(U)
, let
\{\alpha_{t}\}_{t=0}^{m}
be the
\gamma
-string such that
i-1=\alpha_{k}
for some
k
; then,

\mathcal{Q}^{*}(h_{ij}(U))=\tilde{h}_{ij}(U)\prod_{t\geq k+1}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{t}+1,\alpha_{t}+1}(U).

(4.29)

Moreover,
\mathcal{Q}:(G,\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}))\dashrightarrow(G,\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}))
is a birational quasi-isomorphism for
G\in\{\SL_{n},\GL_{n}\}
.

Proof.

The statement in Part
1
follows from the invariance properties of
\varphi
- and
c
-functions (see (
1.6
) and (
1.7
)). For Part
2
, notice that for any
1\leq j\leq n
and any
I\subseteq[1,n]
of size
|I|=n-j+1
,

\mathcal{Q}^{*}\left(\det\mathcal{F}(U)^{[j,n]}_{I}\right)=\det U^{[j,n]}_{I},

and then invoke the definition of the
h
-variables (
1.11
). The last statement can be verified via Proposition
2.25
. The hatted strings
\hat{p}_{ir}
are given by the
c
-variables, which in turn are invariant with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
by Part
1
. To verify that
\mathcal{Q}^{*}(y_{i})=\tilde{y}_{\kappa(i)}
for each nonmarked index
i
, we consider the neighborhoods of the variables as given in Appendix
A
. For instance, from Figure
5
we read that
y(h_{ij})
for
2\leq i\leq j\leq n-1
is given by

y(h_{ij})=\frac{h_{i+1,j}h_{i-1,j-1}h_{i,j+1}}{h_{i-1,j}h_{i,j-1},h_{i+1,j+1}}.

(4.30)

It follows from (
4.29
) that for any
2\leq j\leq n-1
and any
2\leq s,k\leq j
,

\mathcal{Q}^{*}\left(\frac{h_{kj}}{h_{sj}}\right)=\frac{\tilde{h}_{kj}}{\tilde{h}_{sj}}.

(4.31)

Therefore,
\mathcal{Q}^{*}(y(h_{ij}))=y(\tilde{h}_{ij})
. A similar reasoning applies to all the other neighborhoods. By Proposition
2.25
, we conclude that
\mathcal{Q}
is a quasi-isomorphism. To verify that
\mathcal{Q}
is a birational quasi-isomorphism, as in Definition
2.27
, we need to verify that
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
induces an isomorphism

\mathcal{Q}^{*}:\mathcal{O}(\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C}))[h_{ii}^{\pm 1}\ |\ i\in[2,n]]\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}(\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C}))[\tilde{h}_{ii}^{\pm 1}\ |\ i\in[2,n]].

(4.32)

From the defining formula (
4.26
), the formulas from (
C.1
) for the entries of
U_{-}
and the formula (
4.29
), we see that

\mathcal{Q}^{*}\left(\mathcal{O}(\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C}))[{h}_{ii}^{\pm 1}\ |\ i\in[2,n]]\right)\subseteq\mathcal{O}(\gl_{n}(\mathbb{C}))[\tilde{h}_{ii}^{\pm 1}\ |\ i\in[2,n]].

(4.33)

Since
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
is also an isomorphism of the groups of invertible elements of the both localizations, we conclude that (
4.33
) is, in fact, an equality. Thus
\mathcal{Q}
is a birational quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Equivariance property of
\mathcal{Q}
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}^{s}
be the Cartan subalgebra of
\sll_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
Recall that the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}^{s}
is given by

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}:=\{h\in\mathfrak{h}^{s}\ |\ \alpha(h)=\beta(h)\ \text{if}\ \gamma^{j}(\alpha)=\beta\ \text{for some}\ j\}.

(4.34)

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
be the connected subgroup of
\SL_{n}
that corresponds to
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
.

Proposition 4.16
.

For any
T\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
and any
U\in\GL_{n}
,
\mathcal{Q}(TUT^{-1})=T\mathcal{Q}(U)T^{-1}
.

Proof.

We see that

T^{-1}\rho(TUT^{-1})=\prod_{i\geq 1}({\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*})^{i-1}(T^{-1}{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T))({\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*})^{i}(U_{-}).

(4.35)

It suffices to show that
T^{-1}{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)
commutes with
{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(U_{-})
. Indeed, we can decompose
T^{-1}{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)
as

T^{-1}{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)=\mathring{\Pi}_{\Gamma_{1}}(T^{-1}{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T))\mathring{\Pi}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}(T^{-1}{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T)).

(4.36)

Evidently, the second factor commutes with
{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(U_{-})
; as for the first factor, we see that

\mathring{\Pi}_{\Gamma_{1}}(T^{-1}{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}^{*}(T))=\exp\left(\sum_{\Delta}I_{\Delta}t_{\Delta}\right)

(4.37)

where the sum is over nontrivial
X
-runs
\Delta
,
I_{\Delta}:=\sum_{i\in\Delta}e_{ii}
and
t_{\Delta}\in\mathbb{C}
are some scalars. Clearly, the last expression also commutes with
{{\mathring{\phantom{\gamma}}\kern-6.3pt\tilde{\gamma}}}^{*}(U_{-})
. Thus the statement holds.
∎

4.4
Birational quasi-isomorphisms
\mathcal{G}
for
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}

In this subsection, given a pair of BD triples
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}:=(\tilde{\Gamma}_{1},\tilde{\Gamma}_{2},\theta)
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}:=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
of type
A_{n-1}
, we construct a birational map
\mathcal{G}:(\GL_{n},\pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
from the birational maps
\mathcal{Q}:(\GL_{n},\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}:(\GL_{n},\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(\GL_{n},\pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}^{\dagger})
(the map
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}(U):=\tilde{\rho}(U)^{-1}U\tilde{\rho}(U)
is given by (
4.26
), but relative the BD triple
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
, so we decorate the map itself with a tilde). Proposition
4.17
below is a more detailed version of Proposition
1.7
for
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
. Let us set

G_{0}(U):=\tilde{\theta}^{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-})^{-1}\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}),\ \ U\in\GL_{n},

(4.38)

and let
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
and
\mathcal{F}
be the maps given by (
4.10
) and constructed relative
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, respectively.

Proposition 4.17
.

Given any pair
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
of BD triples of type
A_{n-1}
, set
\mathcal{G}:=\mathcal{Q}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{-1}
. Then
\mathcal{G}
is a birational map given by the following formula:

\mathcal{G}(U)=G(U)^{-1}\cdot U\cdot G(U),\ \ G(U):=G_{0}(U)\prod_{i\geq 1}^{\rightarrow}(\tilde{\gamma}^{*})^{i}(G_{0}(U)).

(4.39)

Moreover, for
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
G\in\{\SL_{n},\GL_{n}\}
,
\mathcal{G}:(G,\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{{\dagger}}(\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}))\dashrightarrow(G,\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}))
is a birational quasi-isomorphism, and if the
r_{0}
parts of
\pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}^{\dagger}
and
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
coincide, then
\mathcal{G}:(G,\pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(G,\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
is a Poisson isomorphism.

In Section
4.5
, we will further refine the construction in the case
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is obtained from
\mathbf{\Gamma}
via removal of a pair of roots. There, we will give a more explicit description of the matrix
G_{0}(U)
.

Proof.

Let us start with the definition
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{Q}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{-1}
and derive formula (
4.39
). Recall from (
4.28
) that

\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{-1}(U)=\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)\tilde{\theta}^{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-})^{-1}=\tilde{\theta}^{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-})U\tilde{\theta}^{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-})^{-1}.

We see that

\mathcal{G}(U)=\rho\left(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{-1}(U)\right)^{-1}\tilde{\theta}^{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-})\cdot U\cdot\tilde{\theta}^{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-})^{-1}\rho\left(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{-1}(U)\right).

(4.40)

Set
G(U):=\tilde{\theta}^{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-})^{-1}\rho\left(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{-1}(U)\right)
. Since
\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(\rho(U))=\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})^{-1}\rho(U)
, we see that

\begin{split}\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(G(U))=&\tilde{\gamma}^{*}\left(\tilde{\theta}^{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-})^{-1}\right)\tilde{\gamma}^{*}\left(\left[\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)\tilde{\theta}^{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-})^{-1}\right]_{-}\right)^{-1}\rho\left(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{-1}(U)\right)=\\
=&\tilde{\gamma}^{*}([\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}])^{-1}\rho(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{-1}(U))=G_{0}(U)^{-1}G(U).\end{split}

(4.41)

Now, formula (
4.39
) for
G(U)
follows from the identity
G_{0}(U)\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(G(U))=G(U)
.

To show that
\mathcal{G}
is a birational quasi-isomorphism, let us consider an increasing chain
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\prec\cdots\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}_{m}
of BD triples
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{i}:=(\Gamma_{1,i},\Gamma_{2,i},\gamma_{i})
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}:=\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
,
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{m}:=\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
|\Gamma_{1,i+1}\setminus\Gamma_{1,i}|=1
,
i\in[1,m-1]
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{i}:(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{i}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{i+1}}^{\dagger})
be defined relative
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{i}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{i+1}
, as above. In Proposition
4.22
below we show that each
\mathcal{G}_{i}:(\GL_{n},\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{i}))\dashrightarrow(\GL_{n},\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{i+1}))
is a birational quasi-isomorphism. In particular, by Proposition
2.25
, each
\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}
sends a nonmarked
y
-variable in the initial extended cluster of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{i})
to a
y
-variable in the initial extended cluster of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{i+1})
. Therefore, the same applies for
\mathcal{G}^{*}
, and by Proposition
2.25
, it is a birational quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

Note that since we did not assume
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\prec\mathbf{\Gamma}
in the derivation of formula (
4.39
), the formula covers both
\mathcal{G}
and its inverse
\mathcal{G}^{-1}
. Explicitly, since
\mathcal{G}^{-1}=\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}\circ\mathcal{Q}^{-1}
, we set

\tilde{G}_{0}(U):=\tilde{\theta}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(U)_{-})^{-1}\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(U)_{-}),\ \ U\in\GL_{n};

(4.42)

then,
\mathcal{G}^{-1}
is given by

\mathcal{G}^{-1}(U)=\tilde{G}(U)U\tilde{G}(U)^{-1},\ \ \tilde{G}(U)=\left[\prod_{i\geq 1}^{\leftarrow}(\tilde{\theta}^{*})^{i}(\tilde{G}_{0}(U))\right]\cdot\tilde{G}_{0}(U).

(4.43)

If
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is the trivial BD triple, one recovers
\mathcal{G}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}^{c}
.

4.5
Birational quasi-isomorphisms
\mathcal{G}
for
|\Gamma_{1}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}|=1

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
be any Belavin-Drinfeld triple of type
A_{n-1}
. Fix any pair of roots
p\in\Gamma_{1}
and
q:=\gamma(p)\in\Gamma_{2}
, and let
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
be obtained from
\mathbf{\Gamma}
via removal of
p
and
q
(see Definition
4.2
). Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
be the birational maps defined by (
4.10
) relative the BD triples
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
, respectively. Let
\mathcal{G}:=\mathcal{Q}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{-1}
be the birational map constructed in Section
4.4
. The objective of this subsection is to further refine formulas (
4.38
) and (
4.39
) (the result is the formula (
4.47
) below). However, we will not use the obtained formulas anywhere else in the paper. Let
\Delta
and
\bar{\Delta}
be the
X
- and
Y
-runs that contain
p
and
q
, respectively. Set

\tilde{\Delta}_{1}:=[p_{-}(\Gamma_{1})+1,p],\ \ \ \tilde{\Delta}_{2}:=[p+1,p_{+}(\Gamma_{1})].

(4.44)

For
1\leq i\leq|\tilde{\Delta}_{2}|
and
1\leq j\leq|\tilde{\Delta}_{1}|
, define the following sets of indices:

J_{ij}:=\begin{cases}\{\bar{\gamma}(p_{-}(\Gamma_{1})+j)\}\cup\left([q+1,n]\setminus\{\bar{\gamma}(p+i)\}\right)\ &\text{if}\ \gamma|_{\Delta}>0;\\
\{\bar{\gamma}(p+i)\}\cup\left([q+1,n]\setminus\{\bar{\gamma}(p_{-}(\Gamma_{1})+j)\}\right)\ &\text{if}\ \gamma|_{\Delta}<0.\\
\end{cases}

(4.45)

Formula (
4.38
) for the matrix
G_{0}(U)
can be refined as follows.

Proposition 4.19
.

Under the setup of the current subsection, let us set

\alpha_{ij}(U):=(-1)^{i+1}\frac{\det[\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)]^{J_{ij}}_{[q+1,n]}}{\det[\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)]_{[q+1,n]}^{[q+1,n]}}.

(4.46)

Then

G_{0}(U)=I+\sum_{i=1}^{|\tilde{\Delta}_{2}|}\sum_{j=1}^{|\tilde{\Delta}_{1}|}\alpha_{ij}(U)e_{p+i,p_{-}(\Gamma_{1})+j}.

(4.47)

Proof.

Due to formula (
4.38
), the only nontrivial part of
G_{0}(U)
is given by the submatrix
[G_{0}(U)]_{\tilde{\Delta}_{2}}^{\tilde{\Delta}_{1}}
. If
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}>0
, we see that

\begin{split}\alpha_{ij}(U)=&(-1)^{i+1}\det\left[\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\right]_{[q+1,n]}^{\{q_{-}(\Gamma_{2})+j\}\cup([q+1,n]\setminus\{q+i\})}=\\
=&\sum_{k\in[1,i]}(-1)^{i+k}\left[\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\right]_{q+k,q_{-}(\Gamma_{2})+j}\cdot\det\left[\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\right]_{[q+1,n]\setminus\{q+k\}}^{[q+1,n]\setminus\{q+i\}}=\\
=&\sum_{k\in[1,i]}\left[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\right)\right]_{p+k,p_{-}(\Gamma_{1})+j}\left[\tilde{\theta}^{*}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\right)^{-1}\right]_{p+i,p+k}=\\
=&G_{0}(U)_{p+i,p_{-}(\Gamma_{1})+j}.\end{split}

In the case of
\gamma|_{\Delta\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0
, let us recall that
\bar{\gamma}
is defined in such a way that
\bar{\gamma}(p)=q+1
(see Section
4.1
). Set
\tau:=q_{+}(\Gamma_{2})-q+j
. We see that

\begin{split}\alpha_{ij}(U)=&(-1)^{i+1}\det\left[\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\right]_{[q+1,n]}^{\{q-i+1\}\cup\left([q+1,n]\setminus\{q_{+}(\Gamma_{2})-j+1\}\right)}=\\
=&(-1)^{\tau+1}\det\left[\,\hskip-3.0pt\left.\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\hskip-4.0pt\right.^{-1}\right]_{[q-i+2,q]\cup\{q_{+}(\Gamma_{2})-j+1\}}^{[q-i+1,q]}=\\
=&(-1)^{\tau+1}\sum_{k=1}^{j}(-1)^{k-1}\det\left[\,\hskip-3.0pt\left.\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\hskip-4.0pt\right.^{-1}\right]_{[q-i+1,q]\setminus\{q-i+1\}}^{[q-i+1,q]\setminus\{q-k+1\}}\left[\,\hskip-3.0pt\left.\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\hskip-4.0pt\right.^{-1}\right]_{q_{+}(\Gamma_{2})-j+1,q-k+1}=\\
=&\sum_{k=1}^{i}\left[\tilde{\theta}^{*}\left(\,\hskip-3.0pt\left.\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\hskip-4.0pt\right.^{-1}\right)\right]_{p+i,p+k}\left[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(U)_{-}\right)\right]_{p+k,p_{-}(\Gamma_{1})+j}=G_{0}(U)_{p+i,p_{-}(\Gamma_{1})+j}.\end{split}

where we also used formula (
4.7
). Thus the result follows.
∎

Remark 4.20
.

The reader might notice similarities between the above formulas and the formulas for birational quasi-isomorphisms
\mathcal{U}
in the case of
D(\GL_{n})
from
[
31
, Section 5.1]
.

We will use the following lemma in order to prove that
\mathcal{G}
is a quasi-isomorphism induced by toric actions (in the sense of Proposition
2.26
), as well as to prove Part
3
of Theorem
1.5
in Section
5.2
.

Lemma 4.21
.

The action of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
upon
\GL_{n}
given by
(a,U)\mapsto a\cdot U
,
a\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
U\in\GL_{n}
, induces a global toric action of rank
1
upon
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\GL_{n})
.

Proof.

To prove that the local toric action is global, we appeal to Proposition
2.19
. Notice that all the variables in the initial extended cluster
\Psi_{0}
of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
are homogeneous polynomials. The weight vector
\omega
of the induced local toric action upon
\Psi_{0}
is given by the polynomial degrees of the variables; however, we assign the value
0
to the components of
\omega
that correspond to the
c
-variables (this ensures that the coefficients
\hat{p}_{ir}
from the proposition are invariant with respect to the toric action). Showing that
B\omega=0
for the initial extended exchange matrix
B
amounts to showing that the
y
-variables in
\Psi_{0}
have polynomial degree
0
. From (
1.3
), we see that

\deg\varphi_{kl}=l-1+\frac{1}{2}(n-k-l+1)(n-k-l+2).

(4.48)

In Section
6.2
, we will also show that for any
\gamma
-string
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=0}^{m}
and each
j\in[\alpha_{i}+1,n]
, the polynomial degree of
h_{\alpha_{i}+1,j}
is given by

\deg h_{\alpha_{i}+1,j}=n-j+1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-i}(n-\alpha_{i+\ell}).

(4.49)

Now, to show that the
y
-variables in
\Psi_{0}
have polynomial degree
0
, we analyze each of the neighborhoods from Section
A.1
