-\frac{\mathring{\gamma}^{*}}{1-\mathring{\gamma}^{*}}\pi_{<}[\nabla_{U}g_{ij},U]=\pi_{<}(U\nabla_{U}g_{ij}).

(7.31)

Together with
[\nabla_{U}\phi_{kl},U]\in\mathfrak{b}_{+}
, expanding the bracket
\{g_{ij},\phi_{kl}\}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
yields formula (
7.30
). Since the map
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}:(\GL_{n},\pi_{\text{std}}^{\dagger})\dashrightarrow(\GL_{n},\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger})
is a Poisson isomorphism (see Proposition
3.15
) and a birational quasi-isomorphism (see Proposition
7.8
), it is enough to verify the log-canonicity for
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}
, and that readily follows from combining the formulas (
7.28
), (
7.29
) and (
7.30
) along with
\pi_{0}(U\nabla_{U}\log g_{ij})=\Delta(i,n)
.
∎

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be a BD triple of type
A_{n-1}
. For any choice of
r_{0}
relative
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, the generalized cluster structure
\mathcal{GC}_{g}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
is compatible with
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

In the initial extended cluster
\Psi_{0}
of
\mathcal{GC}_{g}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
, the log-canonicity between the
\phi
-functions is a consequence of Remark
7.3
, the log-canonicity between the
g
-functions is a consequence of Proposition
7.5
and Lemma
3.14
, and the log-canonicity between the
g
- and the
\phi
-functions is a consequence of Lemma
7.12
. Based on Proposition
2.16
, to verify the compatibility of
\mathcal{GC}_{g}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
with
\pi_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\dagger}
, for any cluster variable
\psi_{1}\in\Psi_{0}
and any variable
\psi_{2}\in\Psi_{0}
, one verifies that

\{\log y(\psi_{1}),\psi_{2}\}=\begin{cases}1\ &\text{if}\ \psi_{1}=\psi_{2}\\
0\ &\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(7.32)

Due to Remark
7.3
, if
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\{\phi_{kl}\ |\ k+l<n\}
, then (
7.32
) holds, and due to Proposition
7.5
and Lemma
3.14
, if
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\{g_{ij}\ |\ 2\leq j\leq i\leq n\}
, (
7.32
) holds as well (note that this covers the case of the marked variables of
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}
as well). By Proposition
3.15
, it remains to verify (
7.32
) in the case
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}}
and 1) when
\psi_{1}
is a
\phi
-function and
\psi_{2}
is a
g
-function; 2) when
\psi_{1}
is a
g
-function and
\psi_{2}
is a
\phi
-function; 3) when
\psi_{1}=\phi_{kl}
for
k+l=n
and
\psi_{2}\in\Psi_{0}
is any variable. This is a direct computation based on formulas (
7.28
), (
7.29
) and (
7.30
) together with an analysis of the neighborhood of
\psi_{1}
provided in Appendix
B
.
∎

Appendix A
Initial quiver for
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})

In this appendix, we describe the initial quiver
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
for
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
. We start with describing the quiver
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
in Section
A.1
, which is exemplified in Figure
7
for
n=5
. In Section
A.2
, we explain how to obtain the quiver
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
from
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
for a nontrivial BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. The procedure of obtaining
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
from
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
consists in adding extra arrows to
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
based on
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, as well as in unfreezing some of the vertices. Alternatively, in Section
A.3
we provide explicit neighborhoods of the vertices of
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
that are different from the corresponding neighborhoods in
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
. For explicit examples of
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
for nontrivial BD triples, see Appendix
D
and the supplementary note
[
33
]
. Throughout the section, we assume that
n\geq 3
.

A.1
The quiver for the trivial BD triple

Below one can find pictures of the neighborhoods of all vertices of the initial quiver of
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}},\GL_{n})
. A few remarks beforehand:

•

The circled vertices are mutable (in the sense of ordinary exchange relations (
2.23
)), the square vertices are frozen, the rounded square vertices may or may not be mutable depending on the indices, and the hexagon vertex is a mutable vertex with a generalized mutation relation (see formula (
1.20
));

•

Since
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1}
are isolated variables, they are not shown on the resulting quiver;

•

For
k=2
and
n>3
, the vertices
\varphi_{1k}
and
\varphi_{k-1,2}
coincide; hence, the pictures provided below suggest that there are two edges pointing from
\varphi_{21}
to
\varphi_{12}
(however, there is only one arrow in
n=3
);

•

When the indices of the
h
-variables are out of range, we use convention (
5.1
).;

•

The initial quiver for
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}},\SL_{n})
is the same as for
\mathcal{GC}_{h}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}},\GL_{n})
except the vertex with
h_{11}
has to be removed.

A.2
The quiver for a nontrivial BD triple (algorithm)

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}:=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
be a nontrivial BD triple. In order to obtain the quiver
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
, one proceeds as follows. First, draw the quiver
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
, as described in Appendix
A.1
. Second, add new arrows as prescribed by the following algorithm:

1)

If
j\in\Gamma_{2}
is such that
j-1\notin\Gamma_{2}
or
\gamma|_{\Delta(i)\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0
, add new arrows as indicated in Figure
8a
;

2)

If
j\in\Gamma_{2}
is such that
j-1\in\Gamma_{2}
and
\gamma|_{\Delta(i)\cap\Gamma_{1}}>0
, add new arrows as indicated in Figure
8b
;

3)

Repeat for all roots in
\Gamma_{1}
.

In the algorithm,
\Delta(i)
denotes the
X
-run that contains
i:=\gamma^{*}(j)
for
j\in\Gamma_{2}
.

a

Case
j-1\notin\Gamma_{2}
or
\gamma|_{\Delta(i)\cap\Gamma_{1}}<0

b

Case
j-1\in\Gamma_{2}
and
\gamma|_{\Delta(i)\cap\Gamma_{1}}>0

Figure 8
:
Additional arrows for
j\in\Gamma_{2}
and
i:=\gamma^{*}(j)
.

Appendix B
Initial quiver for
\mathcal{GC}_{g}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma})

In this appendix, we describe the initial quiver
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
for
\mathcal{GC}^{\dagger}_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
. As in the
h
-convention, we start with describing the quiver
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
in Section
B.1
, which is exemplified in Figure
20
for
n=5
. In Section
B.2
, we explain how to obtain the quiver
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
from
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
for a nontrivial BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. The procedure of obtaining
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
from
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
consists in adding extra arrows to
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
based on
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, as well as in unfreezing some of the vertices. Alternatively, in Section
B.3
we provide explicit neighborhoods of the vertices of
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
that are different from the corresponding neighborhoods in
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\text{std}})
. Some explicit examples of
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
for nontrivial BD triples are given in the supplementary note
[
33
]
. Throughout the section, we assume that
n\geq 3
.

Let us mention that the quiver
Q_{g}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
can be obtained from
Q_{h}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}})
via the following steps:

•

Replace each vertex
\varphi_{kl}
with
\phi_{kl}
,
2\leq k+l\leq n
,
k,l\geq 1
and each
h_{ji}
with
g_{ij}
,
2\leq j\leq i\leq n
;

•

For each
g_{ij}
,
2\leq j\leq i\leq n
, reverse the orientation of the arrows in its neighborhood;

•

For the vertices
\phi_{kl}
with
k+l=n
and
k\geq 2
, add an arrow
\phi_{kl}\rightarrow\phi_{k-1,l+1}
;

•

Remove the arrow
\phi_{1,n-1}\rightarrow g_{11}
.

Appendix C
Determinantal identities

In this appendix, we list various determinantal identities that are used throughout the text.

Gauss decomposition.

Let
U\in\GL_{n}
be a generic element. Decompose
U
as
U=U_{\oplus}U_{-}
where
U_{\oplus}
is an upper triangular and
U_{-}
is a unipotent lower triangular matrix. For
1\leq j\leq i\leq n
, the entries of
U_{\oplus}
,
U_{-}
and their inverses are given by the well-known formulas
(see, e.g.
[
14
]
).

\displaystyle{}_{ji}

\displaystyle=\frac{\det U^{[i,n]}_{\{j\}\cup[i+1,n]}}{\det U^{[i+1,n]}_{[i+1,n]}},

\displaystyle[U_{-}]_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{\det U^{\{j\}\cup[i+1,n]}_{[i,n]}}{\det U^{[i,n]}_{[i,n]}},

(C.1)

\displaystyle[\,\hskip-3.0pt\left.U_{\oplus}\hskip-4.0pt\right.^{-1}]_{ji}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j+i}\frac{\det U^{[j+1,n]}_{[j,n]\setminus\{i\}}}{\det U^{[j,n]}_{[j,n]}},

\displaystyle[\,\hskip-3.0pt\left.U_{-}\hskip-4.0pt\right.^{-1}]_{ij}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i+j}\frac{\det U^{[j,n]\setminus\{i\}}_{[j+1,n]}}{\det U^{[j+1,n]}_{[j+1,n]}}.

The next two identities are needed in the derivation of formulas for minors of
\tilde{\gamma}^{*}(U_{-})
. They are used in Section
4.5
for deriving a more explicit formula for birational quasi-isomorphisms, as well as in Sections
6.1
-
6.2
for deriving explicit formulas for the
h
-variables.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
U\in\GL_{n}
be a generic element,
[a,b]\subseteq[1,n]
,
J\subseteq[1,n]
be nontrivial subintervals such that
\max J\leq b
and
|J|=|[a,b]|
. Then

\det[U_{-}]_{[a,b]}^{J}=\frac{\det U^{J\cup[b+1,n]}_{[a,n]}}{\det U^{[a,n]}_{[a,n]}}.

(C.2)

Proof.

The identity follows from an application of the Cauchy-Binet formula to
U_{\oplus}U_{-}
.
∎

Lemma C.2
.

Let us set
\Delta:=[p+1,p+k]\subseteq[1,n]
,
I:=[a,b]\subseteq\Delta
with
b:=p+k
,
J\subseteq\Delta
a subset such that
|J|=|[a,b]|
. Let
\hat{I}:=[\hat{a},\hat{b}]
and
\hat{J}
be the subintervals of
[1,k]
obtained as images of
I
and
J
under the unique increasing bijection
\Delta\rightarrow[1,k]
. Set
w_{0}:\Delta\rightarrow\Delta
and
\hat{w}_{0}:[1,k]\rightarrow[1,k]
to be the unique decreasing bijections, and let
W_{0}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{i-1}e_{k-i+1,i}
. For a generic
U\in\GL_{n}
, the following identity holds:

\det\left[W_{0}[(U_{-})^{-T}]_{\Delta}^{\Delta}W_{0}^{-1}\right]_{\hat{I}}^{\hat{J}}=\frac{\det U^{w_{0}(\Delta\setminus J)\cup[b+1,n]}_{[w_{0}(a)+1,n]}}{\det U^{[w_{0}(a)+1,n]}_{[w_{0}(a)+1,n]}}.

(C.3)

Proof.

It follows from the Cauchy-Binet formula that

\begin{split}\det[W_{0}&[(U_{-})^{-T}]_{\Delta}^{\Delta}W_{0}^{-1}]_{\hat{I}}^{\hat{J}}=(-1)^{\varepsilon}\det\left[[U^{T}_{\oplus}U^{-T}]_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\right]_{[1,\hat{w_{0}}(\hat{a})]}^{\hat{w_{0}}(\hat{J})}=(-1)^{\varepsilon}\det[U^{T}_{\oplus}U^{-T}]_{[p+1,w_{0}(a)]}^{w_{0}(J)}=\\
=&(-1)^{\varepsilon}\det[U^{T}_{\oplus}U^{-T}]_{[1,w_{0}(a)]}^{[1,p]\cup w_{0}(J)}=(-1)^{\varepsilon}\frac{\det U}{\det U^{[w_{0}(a)+1,n]}_{[w_{0}(a)+1,n]}}\det[U^{-T}]_{[1,w_{0}(a)]}^{[1,p]\cup w_{0}(J)}=\\
=&(-1)^{\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime}}\frac{\det U^{w_{0}(\Delta\setminus J)\cup[b+1,n]}_{[w_{0}(a)+1,n]}}{\det U^{[w_{0}(a)+1,n]}_{[w_{0}(a)+1,n]}}.\end{split}

where we introduced

\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon:=\sum_{j\in\hat{w}_{0}(\hat{J})}(j-1)+\sum_{j\in\hat{w}_{0}(\hat{I})}(j-1),\\
\varepsilon^{\prime}:=\sum_{i=w_{0}(a)+1}^{n}i+\sum_{i\in w_{0}(\Delta\setminus J)}i+\sum_{i=p+k+1}^{n}i.\end{array}

A simple computation shows that
\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime}\equiv 0\mod 2
. Thus the formula holds.
∎

Desnanot-Jacobi identity.

The following identity is used in Section
5.1
.

Proposition C.3
.

Let
A
be an
m\times m
matrix with entries in an arbitrary field. If
1\leq i<j\leq m
and
1\leq k<l\leq m
, then the following identity holds:

\det A\det A^{\hat{i}\hat{j}}_{\hat{k}\hat{l}}+\det A_{\hat{l}}^{\hat{i}}\det A_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{j}}=\det A^{\hat{i}}_{\hat{k}}\det A_{\hat{l}}^{\hat{j}},

where the hatted upper (lower) indices indicate that the corresponding column (row) is removed.

Derivatives of flag minors.

For
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
, let us set

\hat{h}_{ij}(U):=\det U_{[i,n-j+i]}^{[j,n]},\ \ U\in\GL_{n}.

(C.4)

In our conventions,
\nabla_{U}^{R}f=U\cdot\nabla_{U}f
and
\nabla_{U}^{L}f=\nabla_{U}f\cdot U
,
U\in\GL_{n}
, where
\nabla_{U}f
is given by formula (
1.25
). The following identities are used in Sections
5.2
-
5.3
:

Lemma C.4
.

The following formula holds:

\langle\pi_{>}\nabla_{U}^{R}\log\hat{h}_{ij},\nabla_{U}^{R}\log\hat{h}_{ks}\rangle=\max\{\min\{i-k,n-s+1\}-\max\{j-s,0\},0\}.

(C.5)

Proof.

It is a matter of a direct computation that

(\nabla^{R}_{U}\hat{h}_{ij})_{qr}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{r-i}\det U^{[j,n]}_{\{q\}\cup([i,n-j+i]\setminus\{r\})}&\text{if}\ q<i\ \text{and}\ r\in[i,n-j+i];\\
(-1)^{n-j+i-r}\det U^{[j,n]}_{([i,n-j+i]\setminus\{r\})\cup\{q\}}&\text{if}\ q>n-j+i\ \text{and}\ r\in[i,n-j+i];\\
\hat{h}_{ij}\ &\text{if}\ q=r\ \text{and}\ r\in[i,n-j+i];\\
0\ &\text{if}\ r\notin[i,n-j+i]\ \text{or}\ q\in[i,n-j+i]\setminus\{r\}.\end{cases}

We see that

\begin{split}\langle\pi_{>}\nabla_{U}^{R}\hat{h}_{ij},&\nabla^{R}_{U}\hat{h}_{ks}\rangle=\\
=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(q,r):\,q\in[k,\min\{i-1,n-s+k\}]\\
r\in[\max\{i,n-s+k+1\},n-j+i]\end{subarray}}(-1)^{n-s+k-q+r-i}\det U^{[j,n]}_{\{q\}\cup([i,n-j+i]\setminus\{r\})}\det U^{[s,n]}_{([k,n-s+k]\setminus\{q\})\cup\{r\}}.\end{split}

Now, the above sum simplifies via a long Plücker relation and is proportional to the product
\hat{h}_{ij}\hat{h}_{ks}
. A case-by-case analysis shows that the proportionality constant is given by (
C.5
).
∎

Lemma C.5
.

The following identities hold:

\pi_{0}\left(\nabla_{U}^{R}\log\hat{h}_{ij}\right)=\sum_{k=i}^{n-j+i}e_{kk},\ \ \pi_{0}\left(\nabla_{U}^{L}\log\hat{h}_{ij}\right)=\sum_{k=j}^{n}e_{kk}.

(C.6)

Proof.

The identities follow from the fact that, given
T=\diag(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
,

\hat{h}_{ij}(TU)=\hat{h}_{ij}(U)\prod_{k=i}^{n-j+i}t_{k},\ \ \hat{h}_{ij}(UT)=\hat{h}_{ij}(U)\prod_{k=j}^{n}t_{k}.\qed

g
-convention) can be found in the supplementary note

]
.
